About / Methodology

About this wiki

How this dataset was built, where the data comes from, and what's still missing.

Overview

This is a living, automated-crawl dataset of every public and private library in the United States, plus every federal, state, county, city, tribal, and special-district government website. Each record carries address, geocoordinates, website URL, funding information, contact details, review ratings, hours of operation, services offered, and area demographics. Every link is verified live, and the dataset is continuously enriched via a scheduled pipeline.

Primary Data Sources

Known Gaps (honest accounting)

  • 14.5% of public library systems (1,339) have no website — almost all are tiny rural/bookmobile libraries with no online presence.
  • 54.1% of public libraries remain unrated — Google Places API daily quota limits accumulation; Gemini LLM fills in well-known libraries but correctly omits obscure branches.
  • 25.6% of gov sites are not live — many are genuinely expired/dead .gov domains (DNS won't resolve).
  • Special libraries (law, medical, theological, corporate) are underrepresented — only 75 added beyond the 3,695 academic libraries.
  • Cron automation is set up but not yet active — run bash automation/setup_cron.sh to enable continuous enrichment.

Update Frequency

The pipeline is designed to run every 30 minutes via cron (automation/run_pipeline.sh). Each run is idempotent and resumable — it picks up where the last run left off, using caches in data/_cache/. Google Places API quota resets daily at midnight PT, so ratings accumulate across runs. The wiki can be rebuilt at any time by re-running python3 wiki/build_wiki.py.

Library of Congress

The largest library in the world; the research arm of the U.S. Congress and the home of the U.S. Copyright Office. Established April 24, 1800. Oldest federal cultural institution in the United States. An agency of the U.S. legislative branch.

Key Findings

  • 20,890 book censorship challenges were documented across the US (2018–2025), resulting in 6,875 bans or removals and 1,332 restrictions.
  • Challenges were reported in 50 states plus DC; Florida (FL) leads with 5,798 challenges.
  • School libraries account for the vast majority of challenges (17,421 of 19,342 in the ALA state report), vs 1,533 in public libraries.
  • The challenge wave peaked in 2023 with 8,466 challenges, after accelerating from ~1,782 (2021) to ~4,465 (2022) to ~8,466 (2023).
  • The most frequently challenged book is 'Gender Queer: A Memoir' (190 challenges); the most challenged author is Hopkins, Ellen (976 challenges).

Intellectual Freedom: The Core Value of Librarianship

Intellectual freedom — the right to access information and ideas without restriction — is a core value of the library profession. The American Library Association's Library Bill of Rights, first adopted in 1939, establishes the principles that libraries should provide materials representing all viewpoints and resist censorship. In an era of unprecedented book challenges, these principles are

    COVID-19 Impact & Recovery

    The COVID-19 pandemic caused the sharpest disruption in the history of American public libraries. This section tracks the decline and partial recovery through FY2024.

    41.6%
    Visit Drop (FY19-FY20)

    Recovery Timeline

    YearVisitsvs BaselineCirculationvs BaselineProgramsvs Baseline
    FY20191254M100.0%2186M100.0%5.9M100.0%
    FY2020732M58.4%1640M75.0%3.6M60.5%
    FY2022671M53.5%1829M83.7%3.3M56.1%
    FY2023803M64.0%1963M89.8%4.6M77.0%

    Key Findings

    • COVID-19 caused the sharpest one-year drop in US public library usage on record: visits fell 41.6% (FY2019 -> FY2020) and circulation fell 25.0%.
    • Despite the FY2020 trough, library income actually rose 2.1% in FY2020 thanks to pre-pandemic budgets and federal relief.
    • By FY2024, visits had recovered to 69.3% of baseline and programs to 85.8% -- meaningful but incomplete recovery.
    • Circulation remains below pre-pandemic levels (78.0% of baseline), reflecting a lasting shift toward digital lending.
    • Library income grew 24.8% from FY2019 to FY2024, outpacing usage recovery as federal relief (CARES, ARPA, IMLS ARP) bolstered budgets.

    Key Findings

    • The American Library Association, founded October 6, 1876 in Philadelphia, is the oldest and largest library association in the world, with approximately 50,000 personal and organizational members.
    • ALA comprises seven active divisions (AASL, ACRL, ALSC, Core, PLA, RUSA, United for Libraries), eleven round tables, and five ethnic affiliates (AILA, APALA, BCALA, CALA, REFORMA).
    • In 2020 ALA restructured: ALCTS, LITA, and LLAMA merged to form the new Core: Leadership, Infrastructure, Futures division, and ASCLA was absorbed into RUSA.
    • ALA's annual operating budget is on the order of $50-60 million, supported by dues, publishing, conferences, grants, and the ALA Endowment; its Washington, D.C. Public Policy and Advocacy office lobbies Congress and federal agencies.
    • The ALA Annual Conference is the world's largest library conference; recent in-person attendance (~15,000-17,000) remains below pre-pandemic peaks above 25,000.
    • All 50 states plus the District of Columbia have their own library associations, most founded between 1886 and 1920; combined personal membership across state associations is approximately 59,000.

    Pct Libraries Free Job Databases

    Specific Program Impacts

    Beyond core lending and reference, libraries run targeted programs whose social impact is well documented. These include the USDA Summer Food Service Program (feeding children when school is out), Wi-Fi hotspot lending, 'Library of Things' collections, seed libraries, tool libraries, maker

    • Summer Food Service Program — Libraries partner with USDA and school districts to serve free meals and snacks to children in low-income areas during summer, when school meal programs are unavailable.
    • Wifi Hotspot Lending — Patrons check out mobile hotspots for home internet access, directly addressing the home broadband gap.
    • Library Of Things — Libraries lend non-traditional items - tools, kitchen equipment, instruments, museum passes, telescopes, assistive devices, and more - reducing consumption and expanding access to goods people cannot
    • Seed Libraries — Patrons borrow seeds, grow plants, and (ideally) save and return seeds, supporting food security, biodiversity, and gardening.
    • Tool Libraries — Some libraries and library-affiliated organizations lend hand and power tools, enabling home repair and DIY for residents who cannot afford to purchase tools.
    • Makerspaces — Public access to 3D printers, laser cutters, CNC machines, sewing, electronics, and coding tools, democratizing access to fabrication technology.
    • Bookmobiles And Mobile Services — Mobile libraries extend service to rural residents, nursing homes, schools, childcare centers, and geographically isolated communities that cannot easily reach a fixed branch.

    Key Studies on Library Impact

    YearSourceKey Finding
    201077 million Americans age 14+ (33%) used a library computer or Wi-Fi network in the prior year; education, employment, and health were leading uses.
    American Library Association92% of libraries provide job resources, 76% help with online job applications, 96% assist with e-government; 62% of unemployed Americans used librarie
    2004Florida public libraries returned $6.54 for every $1 invested, the highest documented library ROI.
    2010Pennsylvania Department of Education$5.50 return per $1 invested.
    2005University of South Carolina$2.86 direct and $4.50 total economic impact per $1 invested.
    2008NorthStar Economics, Inc.$4.06 return per $1; quantified direct, indirect, and induced effects.
    1996Students lose about one month of overall learning and two to three months of reading achievement over summer; losses largest for low-income students.
    2007Two-thirds of the ninth-grade socioeconomic achievement gap is explained by unequal summer learning.
    Pew Research CenterAbout 90% of Americans say public libraries are important to their communities; parents and lower-income Americans rely more heavily on libraries for
    Institute of Museum and Library ServicesNational data on visits, circulation, programs, staffing, and funding; FY2024 recorded about 869 million visits, 1.7 billion circulation, and 5.09 mil
    Network of the National Library of Medicine / NLM / NIHSeven regional offices partner with public libraries on consumer health information, MedlinePlus, and health-literacy programming.
    2009First full-time library social worker (Leah Esguerra) hired, pioneering humane, clinical services for unhoused patrons; replicated in dozens of cities

    Key Findings

    • Public libraries return between $3.00 and $6.54 for every $1 invested, making them among the highest-return public investments measured (state ROI studies 2004-2018).
    • 77 million Americans age 14+ used a library computer or Wi-Fi network in a single year; education (44%), employment (40%), and health (37%) were the leading uses (U.S. IMPACT Study 2010).
    • Libraries directly employ roughly 370,560 workers and operate on about $14.1 billion in annual expenditure while generating an estimated $40-45 billion in community value.
    • 92% of public libraries provide job-seeking resources and 76% help with online job applications, making libraries the most widespread free workforce infrastructure in the nation (PLFTAS).
    • Research shows low-income students lose two to three months of reading each summer; library summer reading programs are the most widely available community response, and two-thirds of the ninth-grade achievement gap traces to unequal summer learning.
    • Library use is remarkably equitable across racial groups (58-72% usage); Black and Hispanic Americans use library computers at higher rates than white Americans, underscoring libraries' role in bridging access gaps.
    • About 90% of public libraries offer free meeting space; roughly 2,000+ serve as passport acceptance facilities, making libraries core civic infrastructure.
    • San Francisco Public Library pioneered the social-worker-in-libraries model in 2009; dozens of cities now embed clinical social services for unhoused patrons.

    Reading & Library Visits: The Long Decline

    The NEA Survey of Public Participation in the Arts (SPPA) documents a troubling long-term decline in both reading and library visitation. Between 2012 and 2022, the percentage of Americans reading any book fell from 54.6% to 48.5%, while novel/short story reading dropped from 45.2% to 37.6%. Simultaneously, IMLS data shows library visits per capita fell from 3.85 in FY2019 to 2.53 in FY2024 — a 34

    YearRead Any BookNovels/Short StoriesPoetryPlays

    202248.5%37.6%9.2%2.4%

    Key Facts

    • The percentage of Americans reading any book declined from 54.6% in 2012 to 48.5% in 2022 — a 6.1 percentage point drop in a decade (NEA SPPA).
    • Novel and short story reading fell from 45.2% (2012) to 37.6% (2022), a decline of 7.6 points.
    • Poetry reading spiked to 11.7% in 2017 (up from 6.7% in 2012) before falling back to 9.2% in 2022.
    • Play reading has remained consistently low: 2.9% (2012), 3.7% (2017), 2.4% (2022).
    • IMLS per-capita library visits fell from 3.85 (FY2019) to 2.01 (FY2022) — a 48% decline — before partially recovering to 2.53 in FY2024.

    Book Format Shift: Print, E-Books & Audiobooks

    Over 14 years, Americans' reading format preferences shifted dramatically. Print declined from 72% to 64%, while e-books more than doubled from 17% to 31% and audiobooks more than tripled from 11% to 26%. Overall reading remained stable at ~75%.

    Key Facts

    • Print book readership declined from 72% (2011) to 64% (2025), an 8-point drop.
    • E-book readership more than doubled from 17% (2011) to 31% (2025).
    • Audiobook readership more than tripled from 11% (2011) to 26% (2025).
    • Overall reading remained stable at ~75%.
    • The e-book surge reflects Kindle, Libby/OverDrive, and library digital lending.

    Public Attitudes Toward Libraries (Pew Research)

    Americans overwhelmingly value their public libraries. Majorities say libraries provide the resources they need, and two-thirds say closing their local library would have a major impact on their community.     Key Facts

    • 77% of Americans say libraries provide the resources they need (Pew 2016).
    • 66% say closing their local public library would have a major impact on their community (Pew 2016), up from 63% in 2013.
    • 69% say libraries contribute a lot as a safe place to spend time, and 58% say they provide educational opportunities.
    • 80% of Americans say libraries should definitely teach digital skills, showing high expectations for library tech services.
    • Despite valuing libraries, only 31% say books should definitely not be moved to make room for tech spaces, while 24% support it and 40% are open to it.

    Library Services for Special & Underserved Populations

    Reference compilation of U.S. public-library services for ten special and underserved populations: people with disabilities, veterans, immigrants and refugees, people experiencing homelessness, incarcerated people, seniors, children and youth, Indigenous/Native American communities, LGBTQ+ communities, and rural populations. Each section summarizes key services, notable programs and examples, fund

    10
    Populations Served

    People with Disabilities

    Public and national libraries serve people with visual, physical, and reading disabilities primarily through the Library of Congress National Library Service for the Blind and Print Disabled (NLS) and its cooperating network, complemented by local ADA compliance, assistive technology, accessible col

    Veterans

    Libraries serve veterans through partnerships with the Department of Veterans Affairs (VA), the Library of Congress Veterans History Project, state library veteran-resource programs, PTSD and mental-health collections, veteran job fairs, and benefits-navigation assistance.

    • VA partnerships & benefits navigation — Libraries host VA benefits information, partner with VA medical centers and Vet Centers, and connect veterans to claims assistance.
    • Veterans Connect @ the Library — California State Library program (funded through LSTA/IMLS) providing veteran resource toolkits and training to public libraries; a widely replicated
    • VetNow / Brainfuse VetNow — Online veteran benefits, transition, and job-search assistance offered by libraries through the Brainfuse platform.
    • PTSD & mental-health resources — Curated collections and programming drawing on the VA National Center for PTSD; some libraries host veteran peer-support and reading groups.
    • Veteran job & resource fairs — Libraries host hiring events, resume workshops, and veteran-resource fairs, often with workforce boards and employers.
    • Oral-history recording — Libraries train volunteers to record veteran oral histories for submission to the VHP.

    Immigrants & Refugees

    Libraries are frontline service providers for immigrants and refugees, offering English-language learning, citizenship-preparation resources, multilingual collections, naturalization ceremony hosting, and referrals to legal-aid partners - frequently through partnerships with USCIS and IMLS.

    • English as a Second Language (ESL) classes — Free ESL classes, conversation circles, one-on-one tutoring, and digital-literacy support for newcomers.
    • Citizenship Corners / New Americans Corners — Designated spaces with naturalization study materials, citizenship test prep, and USCIS resources; launched through the USCIS-IMLS partnership beginni
    • Naturalization ceremonies — Libraries serve as naturalization ceremony venues, especially around Constitution Day and Citizenship Day (September 17).
    • Multilingual collections — Books, media, and e-resources in Spanish, Chinese, Vietnamese, Arabic, and other community languages.
    • Naturalization test preparation — Civics and English test prep classes, 'Preparing for the Oath' (USCIS) study materials, and mock interviews.
    • Immigration legal help — Libraries host immigration legal clinics and refer patrons to DOJ-accredited representatives and EOIR-recognized organizations; libraries themselves d

    People Experiencing Homelessness

    Public libraries are among the most accessible daytime refuges for people experiencing homelessness. A growing number of urban systems employ or contract social workers, peer-support specialists, and outreach staff, and provide hygiene services, mailing-address assistance, and referrals to housing a

    • Library social workers — On-staff or contracted licensed social workers providing crisis de-escalation, benefits enrollment, and service referrals; pioneered by San Francisco
    • Hygiene services — Restroom access, personal-care supplies, and in some cities mobile showers (e.g., SFPL's partnership with Lava Mae) and laundry support.
    • Mailing-address services — Some libraries permit patrons to use the library as a mailing address for IDs, benefits, and correspondence - a critical barrier-reducer for housing a
    • Homeless outreach / peer support — Peer outreach workers with lived experience connect patrons to shelters, housing navigators, and mental-health and substance-use services.
    • Library card & ID access — Flexible card policies for people without fixed addresses; assistance obtaining state IDs.
    • Drop-in daytime refuge — Libraries function as de facto day shelters offering climate-controlled space, seating, charging, and internet.

    Seniors

    Libraries serve older adults through homebound delivery, large-print and accessible collections, senior digital-literacy help, memory cafes and dementia-friendly programming, and partnerships with senior centers and organizations such as AARP.

    • Homebound delivery — 'Books by Mail' and volunteer-driven home delivery of materials to homebound seniors and people in assisted living/nursing facilities.
    • Large-print collections — Widely available large-print books for low-vision readers.
    • Senior tech help / digital literacy — One-on-one device help, smartphone/computer classes, and partners such as AARP, Senior Planet (OATS), and OASIS Connections.
    • Memory cafes — Dementia-friendly social gatherings for people with memory loss and their care partners; model originated in the Netherlands (Dr. Bere Miesen, 1997) a
    • Dementia-friendly programs — Staff training in dementia-friendly service, sensory and reminiscence programming, and partnerships with the Alzheimer's Association.
    • Senior partnerships & benefits help — AARP Tax-Aide tax-preparation sites, Medicare/Social Security information, and senior-center collaborations.

    Children & Youth

    Youth services are the most universal and well-developed library program area, spanning early literacy, storytime, summer reading and learning, STEM/STEAM, teen spaces, homework help, and - increasingly - summer meals that pair learning with food security.

    • Early literacy — Every Child Ready to Read (ALA/PLA, 2nd edition) promotes five practices - talk, sing, read, write, play - to build pre-reading skills from birth.
    • Storytime — Baby, toddler, preschool, and family storytimes building language and school-readiness; a near-universal public-library offering.
    • Summer reading / summer learning — Collaborative Summer Library Program (CSLP) and state programs combat the 'summer slide' with reading incentives and programming.
    • STEM / STEAM — Coding, robotics, maker spaces, and informal science; NASA@MyLibrary (IMLS/NASA partnership via STAR Net / Space Science Institute) brings STEM to pub
    • Teen spaces & services — Dedicated teen areas, teen advisory boards, and TeenTober (merged Teen Tech Week + Teen Read Week) led by YALSA.
    • Homework help — After-school programs and online tutoring (e.g., Brainfuse Homework Help) provided by many systems.

    Indigenous / Native American

    Tribal libraries serve Native communities as cultural, language, and information hubs. IMLS administers dedicated Native American Library Services grants, and the Association of Tribal Archives, Libraries, and Museums (ATALM) supports the field. Language preservation and cultural-revitalization prog

    • Tribal library services — Tribally operated libraries providing collections, cultural programming, and community information to Native communities.
    • Language preservation — Digitization and teaching of Native languages, creation of language materials, and revitalization projects (e.g., Cherokee syllabary, Navajo/Dine, Oji
    • Cultural & archival collections — Tribal archives, oral histories, photographs, and culturally appropriate stewardship of Indigenous knowledge.
    • Digital inclusion — Tribal libraries as broadband and technology access points; supported by IMLS and tribal broadband programs.

    LGBTQ+ Community

    Libraries serve LGBTQ+ communities through pride collections, Drag Storytime, dedicated LGBTQ+ archives, safe-space training, and award lists. The ALA Rainbow Round Table administers the Stonewall Book Awards, and LGBTQ+ titles are heavily represented among the most frequently challenged books.

    • Pride collections — Dedicated LGBTQ+ fiction and nonfiction collections, often highlighted during Pride Month (June).
    • Drag Storytime / Drag Queen Story Hour — Drag performers read inclusive stories to children; originated ~2015 at Brooklyn Public Library with Drag Queen Story Hour NYC. Programs have drawn bo
    • LGBTQ+ archives — Dedicated archives documenting LGBTQ+ history; notable examples include the James C. Hormel LGBTQIA Center at SFPL and the ONE National Gay & Lesb
    • Safe-space programs — Staff training, safe-space signage, pronoun/name practices, and inclusive programming signals.
    • Award lists & readers' advisory — ALA Rainbow Book List and Stonewall Book Awards guide collection development and readers' advisory.

    Rural Communities

    Rural and small libraries serve low-population communities through bookmobiles, mail-order service, satellite branches, and as critical broadband and social-service access points. The Association for Rural & Small Libraries (ARSL) and federal connectivity programs (E-Rate, BEAD, ACP) are central

    • Bookmobiles — Mobile library vehicles serving remote communities, schools, and stops without a fixed branch; a hallmark of rural service, though fleets have decline
    • Mail-order library service — 'Books by Mail' programs shipping materials to rural patrons who live far from any branch.
    • Satellite branches — Small fixed outlets and storefront branches extending service across geographically large, low-density service areas.
    • Broadband & internet access — Rural libraries as primary public internet access points; supported by the FCC E-Rate program, the BEAD broadband-deployment program, and the Affordab
    • Social-service hub — In small communities the library often functions as the sole public computing, printing, notary, government-forms, and gathering space.

    Key Findings

    • Libraries increasingly function as community social-service hubs, employing social workers, veteran navigators, peer-support specialists, and benefits-access staff - roles historically outside librarianship.
    • Federal funding is foundational but uneven: IMLS Grants to States (LSTA), E-Rate, BEAD/ACP, USDA Rural Development, OSEP (Bookshare), and targeted grant programs (Native American Library Services, NASA@MyLibrary) support special-population services, yet most depend on local initiative.
    • The NLS/BARD program is the flagship federal service for print disabilities but reaches only a fraction of eligible readers due to under-enrollment, certification barriers, and device/connectivity gaps.
    • Equity programs blend library service with civic and government functions: USCIS Citizenship Corners for immigrants, the Library of Congress Veterans History Project, and the proposed Prison Libraries Act (H.R. 7247) all situate libraries in naturalization, veteran commemoration, and corrections.
    • Rural and small libraries - a majority of U.S. systems by count - face compounded connectivity, staffing, and budget gaps while serving as the sole public computing and gathering space in many communities.
    • Youth services are the most universal and well-developed program area (early literacy, summer reading, STEM); they increasingly pair learning with food security via USDA Summer Food Service Program meal sites at libraries.

    Online & Hybrid Programs

    Fully online programs: 33

    Programs with online option: 48

    Diversity Initiatives

    ALA Spectrum Scholarship:

    Ethnic affiliate scholarship programs:

    Key Findings

    • 56 ALA-accredited MLIS programs operate across 35 US states/territories (plus programs in Canada and the UK, for ~65 total in the ALA directory).
    • 32 of the US ALA-accredited programs also offer a doctoral (PhD) program; an additional set of non-ALA-accredited iSchools (UC Berkeley, Cornell, Penn State, etc.) award information-science PhDs.
    • Online delivery now dominates US LIS education: 48 of 56 accredited programs offer an online option, and 33 are fully online only. San Jose State University runs the largest fully-online MLIS cohort.
    • The ALA Spectrum Scholarship (est. 1997) has supported ~1,300+ scholars of color, the single largest diversity-recruitment effort in LIS; it is complemented by AILA, APALA, BCALA, CALA, and REFORMA scholarships.
    • School librarian certification is governed state-by-state; all 51 states/DC listed generally require a teaching certificate plus a master's (often MLIS) and a school library media endorsement/program, though titles and routes vary (e.g., New York 'School Library Media Specialist,' North Carolina 'School Library Media Coordinator,' California 'Teacher Librarian Services Credential').
    • The ALA-APA Library Support Staff Certification (LSSC, est. 2010) and Certified Public Library Administrator (CPLA, est. 2007) are the principal national voluntary credentials; both require continuing education for recertification (30 contact hours per 4-year cycle).
    • Mandatory CE for general public/academic librarians is uncommon; the strongest mandatory-CE structures apply to credentialed school librarians (via teacher-license renewal, typically 60-180 hours per 5-year cycle) and to ALA-APA credential holders.
    • The LIS workforce remains ~83% white (non-Hispanic); LIS students are somewhat more diverse (~72-78% white), reflecting gains from Spectrum and affiliate recruitment, but gender remains ~80%+ female.

    Library Awards & Recognition Programs

    The American library profession recognizes excellence through a rich ecosystem of national awards, state honors, design prizes, and professional recognition programs. The IMLS National Medal is the nation's highest honor for library and museum service, while Library Journal and the ALA administer dozens of awards recognizing literary achievement, professional leadership, and institutional innovation.

    0
    IMLS National Medal recipients
    0
    Medal-winning libraries
    0
    ALA literary awards
    0
    States with medal winners

    Key Findings

    • The IMLS National Medal for Museum and Library Service is the highest federal library honor in the U.S.; 222 museums and libraries received it across 1994-2024 (no medal in 2020), including 95 library recipients and 5 dual museum-and-library institutions across roughly 40 states and territories.
    • California (20), Illinois (16), New York (14), Florida (11), and Pennsylvania (10) have produced the most National Medal recipients, reflecting both the size of their library/museum sectors and the breadth of eligible institutions.
    • Library Journal's annual awards form a coordinated calendar: Librarian of the Year (January), Best Small Library in America (February), Movers & Shakers (March), Library of the Year (June), and the Teaching Award (November).
    • Library Journal's Star Libraries rating (begun 2008) is the modern successor to HAPLR; it rates libraries on four per-capita output measures within expenditure peer groups, whereas HAPLR (1999-c.2010) used a single composite score across all sizes.
    • The ALA Youth Media Awards ecosystem is dominated by youth literature: the Newbery (1922) and Caldecott (1938) are the oldest and most recognized, joined by the Printz (2000, young adult), Coretta Scott King (1970), Belpré (1996), Sibert (2001), Geisel (2006), and Schneider (2004), among more than 20 awards.
    • Frederic G. Melcher single-handedly created the two most famous U.S. children's book awards: he proposed and endowed both the Newbery Medal (1922) and the Caldecott Medal (1938), making him arguably the most influential figure in the history of American children's literature recognition.
    • Many major library awards are named for librarians and advocates whose lives embody the award: Margaret A. Edwards (founder of YA service), John Cotton Dana (public-relations pioneer), Pura Belpré (first Puerto Rican NYPL librarian), and Michael L. Printz (school librarian) -- the hall of fame is thus encoded in the awards themselves.
    • Several libraries appear in multiple recognition systems, signaling sustained excellence: Nashville Public Library (2010 IMLS Medal + 2017 LJ Library of the Year), Columbus Metropolitan Library (2011 IMLS Medal + 2010 LJ Library of the Year + perennial Star Library), and Craig Public Library (2015 IMLS Medal + 2017 Best Small Library).

    Library Law & Governance

    A comprehensive reference to the legal framework governing U.S. public libraries — from state enabling legislation and library district laws to board governance models, reader privacy statutes, intellectual freedom laws, open meeting and public records requirements, employment/certification, and copyright/lending frameworks.

    Minnesota1959Authorizes cities, counties, and regional public library systems; establishes regional system structure.
    Mississippi1964Authorizes counties and municipalities to establish public libraries; establishes Mississippi Library Commission.

    Library District Laws

    Library districts are special-purpose governmental units with independent taxing authority. They represent one of the most powerful funding mechanisms for libraries in the U.S.

    • Independent library district (elected board, taxing authority): Special-purpose political subdivision with elected trustees and voter-approved property-tax authority; the strongest autonomous model.
    • County/parish library district (appointed board, taxing authority): County- or parish-wide library with appointed board and voter-approved millage/levy.
    • Regional library system (appointed board, no independent taxing): Multicounty cooperative funded by member governments and state aid; no separate taxing district.
    • Special district (varied): Authorization exists for special/library districts, but funding mechanisms and autonomy vary widely.

    Board Governance Models

    Copyright & Lending Framework

    First Sale Doctrine (§109): {'codification': '17 U.S.C. § 109(a)', 'origin': 'Bobbs-Merrill Co. v. Straus, 210 U.S. 339 (1908)', 'effect': "Permits the owner of a lawfully made copy to lend, sell, or otherwise dispose of it without the copyright holder's consent; the legal basis for library circulation and interlibrary loan of physical materials."}

    Library Reproduction Exemption (§108): {'codification': '17 U.S.C. § 108', 'scope': "Permits libraries and archives to reproduce works for: (1) preservation/security of their own collections; (2) replacement of damaged/deteriorated/lost/out-of-print works; (3) limited interlibrary loan copies (CONTU 'rule of five'); (4) single copies for patrons at their request under certain conditions; and for the last 20 years of copyright term under § 108(h).", 'limitations': "Does not apply where a replacement copy is available at a reasonable price; 'systematic' copying for interlibrary loan must comply with the CONTU guidelines; no advantage over individually licensed use."}

    Fair Use (§107): {'codification': '17 U.S.C. § 107', 'factors': ['(1) purpose and character of the use (commercial vs. nonprofit educational)', '(2) nature of the copyrighted work', '(3) amount and substantiality used', '(4) effect on the potential market'], 'library_relevance': 'Library and archive digitization, search, accessibility, and reserve uses are routinely evaluated under fair use, per HathiTrust, Google Books, and Georgia State cases.'}

    Digital lending limitations: {'issue': 'First sale does not extend cleanly to digital lending; controlled digital lending (CDL) is contested.', 'key_case': 'Capitol Records v. ReDigi (2d Cir. 2018) rejected digital first sale.', 'practice': 'Libraries use licensed e-book platforms (OverDrive, etc.) rather than relying on § 109 for digital loans.'}

    Interlibrary loan: {'basis': '§ 108(d)-(g) and CONTU guidelines (rule of five: no more than 5 copies of an article title in a calendar year for the prior 5 years).', 'key_case': 'Williams & Wilkins Co. v. United States (1973) upheld interlibrary photocopying as fair use.'}

    Key Court Cases

    CaseYearIssueRuling
    Bobbs-Merrill Co. v. Straus1908Established the first sale doctrine: the copyright owner's distribution right is exhausted after the first sale, enabling libraries to lend books without permission.
    Williams & Wilkins Co. v. United States1973Held that library photocopying for interlibrary loan was fair use, strongly influencing the § 108 CONTU guidelines.
    Harper & Row, Publishers, Inc. v. Nation Enterprises1985Narrowed fair use by emphasizing the fourth factor (market effect); relevant to libraries excerpting copyrighted news/memoir material.
    Board of Education, Island Trees Union Free School District v. Pico1982School boards cannot remove books from school libraries simply because they disagree with the ideas they contain (plurality).
    Kreimer v. Bureau of Police for the Town of Morristown1992Upheld a public library's behavior rules (hygiene, harassment) as reasonable, content-neutral time/place/manner restrictions.
    Bounds v. Smith1977States must provide prisoners with adequate law libraries or legal assistance to access the courts.
    Lewis v. Casey1996Narrowed Bounds, requiring prisoners to show actual injury from inadequate library access, not merely its inadequacy.
    Mainstream Loudoun v. Board of Trustees of Loudoun County Library1998Struck down a public library's mandatory internet filtering policy as overbroad and unconstitutional prior restraint.
    United States v. American Library Association2003Upheld CIPA's filtering requirement for libraries receiving E-Rate funds as constitutional; adults may request unblocking.
    Bradburn v. North Central Regional Library District2010Held that a library may use filtering consistent with CIPA; librarries need not disable filters for adult patrons upon request under the library's policy.
    Authors Guild, Inc. v. HathiTrust2014Held that full-text digitization of books for full-text search and accessible copies for the print-disabled is fair use.
    Authors Guild v. Google, Inc. (Google Books)2015Held that Google Books' snippet-view digitization of library collections is fair use.
    Cambridge University Press v. Patton (Georgia State University e-reserves)2014Provided detailed fair-use framework for academic library electronic course reserves; remanded for case-by-case analysis.
    Capitol Records, LLC v. ReDigi Inc.2018Rejected a digital first sale doctrine for resale of digital music files; reproduction occurs in the transfer.
    Doe v. Gonzales (re Library Connection NSL)2008Library Connection successfully challenged an NSL gag order, leading to disclosure and statutory reform allowing judicial review.

    Key Findings

    • All 50 states and DC have library enabling legislation authorizing the creation of public libraries, but only a subset authorize independent library districts with taxing authority; Colorado, Illinois, Michigan, New York, Oregon, and Washington are leading district states.
    • Library governance divides between appointed boards (the majority) and elected boards (concentrated in library-district states and New England town-meeting states); board size is most commonly 5, 7, or 9 members with staggered three-year terms, though Ohio's seven-year terms are an outlier.
    • Every state has an open meetings law applying to library boards, requiring notice, open deliberation, and minutes, with executive sessions limited to enumerated topics such as personnel and litigation.
    • Every state has a public records / FOI law applying to libraries, but patron circulation and registration records are universally carved out by library-privacy statutes, creating a near-nationwide wall between administrative transparency and reader privacy.
    • Approximately 50 states have some form of library record confidentiality; a handful historically relied on general privacy law or attorney-general opinion before enacting library-specific provisions.
    • Roughly half of states require statewide public librarian certification, typically population-tiered and tied to ALA-accredited MLIS for the largest jurisdictions; states without mandatory certification still commonly require the MLIS at the employer level.
    • The first sale doctrine (Bobbs-Merrill v. Straus, 1908; codified at 17 U.S.C. § 109) is the legal foundation of physical library lending, while § 108 provides libraries and archives reproduction exemptions for preservation, replacement, and interlibrary loan.
    • Digital lending remains constrained: Capitol Records v. ReDigi (2018) rejected a digital first sale doctrine, so libraries rely on licensed e-book platforms rather than § 109 for digital circulation.

    Solar Energy at Libraries

    A growing number of public and academic libraries have installed rooftop or ground-mounted solar photovoltaic (PV) arrays, often funded by a mix of local capital budgets, state energy/renewable-energy grants, federal stimulus and American Rescue Plan funds, utility rebates, and bond measures. Solar reduces operating costs and serves as a visible sustainability demonstration.

    Estimated savings: Reported annual electricity-cost savings vary from a few thousand dollars for small branch arrays to six-figure savings for large central-library systems. Exact figures are library-specific and not centrally aggregated.

    Scale: There is no single national registry of solar installations on libraries; the count is believed to be in the hundreds nationally and growing, driven by falling PV costs and climate goals. This figure is an estimate.

    Fayetteville Public Library
    Reading Public Library
    Various California public libraries (Napa, Sonoma County, etc.)
    Various Massachusetts public libraries

    Seed Libraries

    Seed libraries allow patrons to 'check out' seeds at planting time and 'return' saved seeds at harvest, preserving biodiversity and food security. The movement began around 2010 and spread rapidly across US public libraries.

    Estimated US seed libraries: 500 (Estimates place the number of seed libraries in the US at roughly 500+; there is no single authoritative registry, and counts vary by source. Globally the figure is often cited at 600+.)

    Origin: {'first_public_library_seed_library': 'Richmond Grows Seed Lending Library', 'location': 'Richmond Public Library, Richmond, California', 'founded': 2010, 'significance': 'Widely credited as the first seed library in a US public library; its open-source model (using the library catalog) seeded the broader movement.', 'confidence': 'high'}

    Climate Resilience & Community Hubs

    Libraries are increasingly recognized as community resilience hubs. They are trusted, publicly accessible, have power/AC, internet, and long hours - making them natural cooling centers in heat waves, warming centers in cold snaps, and recovery points after disasters. They also serve as venues for climate education.

    Cooling centers: {'description': "Many cities formally designate libraries as cooling centers during extreme heat events. Libraries' air conditioning, seating, water, Wi-Fi, and extended hours make them ideal, and they protect heat-vulnerable populations (elderly, unhoused, those without home AC).", 'examples': ['New York City designates libraries among its official cooling centers', 'Chicago, Boston, Seattle, Phoenix, and many other cities use libraries as cooling centers', 'Public health agencies coordinate with library systems during heat advisories'], 'confidence': 'high'}

    Warming centers: {'description': 'During extreme cold and winter storms, libraries serve as warming centers, particularly in northern states and for energy-insecure patrons.', 'confidence': 'high'}

    Emergency & disaster recovery: {'description': 'After disasters, libraries become critical recovery hubs: providing internet and device charging, helping people file FEMA/insurance claims, offering a safe workspace, and distributing information.', 'notable_responses': [{'event': 'Hurricane Harvey (2017)', 'location': 'Houston, TX', 'role': 'Houston Public Library branches served as recovery and resource centers after catastrophic flooding; provided internet, charging, and FEMA-filing help.'}, {'event': 'Hurricane Sandy (2012)', 'location': 'NY / NJ', 'role': 'Queens Library and Brooklyn Public Library branches provided power, internet, and recovery assistance; some branches operated as relief sites.'}, {'event': 'Hurricane Katrina (2005)', 'location': 'Gulf Coast', 'role': 'Libraries in affected areas and host communities provided information, internet, and shelter support to displaced residents.'}, {'event': 'Wildfire smoke / power shutoffs (2017-2020s)', 'location': 'California, OR', 'role': 'Libraries served as clean-air respite sites during wildfire smoke events and as charging/resource hubs during PSPS power shutoffs.'}], 'confidence': 'high'}

    Resilience hub model: The 'resilience hub' framework - buildings that provide day-to-day services and scale up during emergencies - aligns naturally with libraries' mission and physical assets; several library systems are formally adopting resilience-hub planning.

    Sustainable Lending Collections

    Beyond books, many libraries lend objects and resources that promote sustainability, self-sufficiency, and a circular economy - reducing consumption by sharing.

      Women in Librarianship

      The feminization of librarianship is one of the defining features of the profession. Women now comprise approximately 80% of the library workforce.

      NameContribution
      Mary Lemist TitcombIntroduced the first U.S. bookwagon (1905).

      African American Library History

      Notable African American Library Pioneers

      • Dorothy Porter Wesley: Bibliographer who built Howard University's Moorland-Spingarn Research Center and pioneered inclusive cataloging.
      • Carla Hayden: First African American and first woman Librarian of Congress (2016-2025).
      • Augusta Braxton Baker: NYPL children's librarian who championed authentic portrayals of Black Americans in children's literature.
      • Thomas Fountain Blue: Head of Louisville's segregated colored branches; among the first African American public library administrators.
      • Regina Andrews: NYPL librarian and playwright; first African American to hold a supervising branch librarian title at NYPL.

      Key Findings

      • U.S. library history begins with private, church, and bequest collections; Captain Robert Keayne's 1656 Boston book bequest is the earliest colonial precursor.
      • Benjamin Franklin's Library Company of Philadelphia (1731) was the first successful subscription library in the colonies and the model for the 'library company' form nationwide.
      • The transition to tax-supported public libraries culminated in the Peterborough, New Hampshire town library (1833, first tax-supported public library) and the Boston Public Library (1848 founded / 1854 opened, first large municipally supported free public library).
      • Boston Public Library achieved multiple U.S. 'firsts': first branch (East Boston, 1870), first to open free to the public (1854), and first dedicated children's room (1895).
      • Andrew Carnegie's library program funded 1,419 public grants producing 1,687 public library buildings plus 109 academic libraries in the United States between 1886 and 1923, totaling about $45.86 million (the Wikipedia 'Totals' row reports $45,865,440.10; the sum of the per-state rows parsed here is $45,864,940.10, a $500 difference from sub-row handling in the source table). Alaska and Delaware received no Carnegie libraries; Pennsylvania received Carnegie's first U.S. grant in 1886.
      • The states receiving the most Carnegie funding were New York ($6,697,149), Pennsylvania ($5,610,587), Ohio ($3,239,928.64), California ($2,836,987), and Indiana ($2,588,664.38). Indiana received the most public grants (156).
      • Melvil Dewey founded the ALA (1876), invented the Dewey Decimal Classification (1876), and established the first U.S. library school (1887); Charles Ammi Cutter produced America's first public card catalog and shaped modern classification.
      • Mary Lemist Titcomb launched the first U.S. bookwagon at Washington County Free Library, Hagerstown, Maryland, in 1905.

      Library Disaster Response & Community Resilience

      Libraries have served as emergency shelters, cooling centers, and community recovery hubs during America's worst natural disasters — from Hurricane Katrina to the Maui wildfires, from Joplin tornadoes to the COVID-19 pandemic.

      Hurricane Responses

      Library Architecture & Building Design

      Library buildings are among America's most significant civic structures — from grand Beaux-Arts palaces of the Gilded Age to the bold contemporary designs of Rem Koolhaas and Moshe Safdie. This section traces the architectural evolution of the American library.

      Architectural Eras

      HurricaneYearLibrary Response
      EraPeriodCharacteristics
      Gilded Age / Beaux-Arts (1880s-1910s)1880-1920['Monumental, symmetrical stone-clad buildings inspired by European palaces and Renaissance models', 'Grand exterior staircases leading to elevated entrances signifying the dignity of knowledge', 'Vaulted, ornately decorated reading rooms with murals, sculpted ceilings, and marble detailing', 'Closed-stack systems with books retrieved by staff; readers consulted catalogs in grand reference halls', 'Beaux-Arts planning principles taught at the Ecole des Beaux-Arts in Paris dominated American practice']
      Carnegie Era (1900s-1920s)1886-1929["Standardized, economical designs funded by Andrew Carnegie's grants (the 'Carnegie formula')", 'Prominent front steps symbolizing elevation of the mind through reading', "Classical-columned porticoes and a 'FREE TO ALL' inscription over the entrance", 'Open-shelf access (a Carnegie requirement) replacing closed staff-retrieved stacks', "Basement-level children's rooms and community assembly space"]
      Mid-Century Modern (1950s-1960s)1950-1970['Clean-lined, functionalist buildings expressing structure and material honestly', 'Generous glazing bringing daylight into reading areas; open floor plans', 'Use of reinforced concrete, steel frames, and curtain-wall systems', 'Departure from ornament; emphasis on flexibility and modular stack layouts', 'Air-conditioned interiors enabling compact, deep-plan buildings']
      Brutalist (1970s)1965-1980['Massive, monolithic forms in poured-in-place concrete (beton brut)', 'Sculptural, sculpted massing and deep-set windows', 'Raw, unadorned surfaces celebrating the weight and permanence of the institution', 'Often criticized for poor thermal performance and foreboding public image']
      Post-Modern (1980s-1990s)1980-2000['Reintroduction of color, ornament, historical reference, and playful forms', 'Use of recognizable motifs (arches, keystones, pediments) in non-literal ways', 'Contextual designs that respond to surrounding historic fabric', 'Emphasis on civic symbolism and readability over pure function']
      Contemporary / Starchitecture Era (2000s-present)2000-present["Signature designs by internationally recognized 'starchitects' as civic landmarks", "Programmatic 'function stacking' separating reading, collection, and public zones", 'Heavy use of glass, daylighting, and visual connection to the street', 'Integration of digital technology, automated storage/retrieval, and maker/community spaces', 'Sustainability goals (LEED certification) and adaptive reuse of historic structures']

      Carnegie Library Architecture

      2,509
      Carnegie libraries worldwide
      1,689
      In the United States

      Between 1883 and 1929, Scottish-American industrialist Andrew Carnegie funded the construction of 2,509 Carnegie libraries worldwide. Of these, 1,689 were built in the United States, with 660 in the United Kingdom and Ireland, 125 in Canada, and others elsewhere. By one count Carnegie's grants funded the construction of nearly half of all libraries in the United States.

      Notable Renovations

      • New York Public Library, Rose Main Reading Room restoration — Preserved one of the most famous interior spaces in the United States while ensuring public safety.
      • Boston Public Library, Johnson Building renovation — A landmark example of transforming a Brutalist civic building into an inviting, daylit public space.
      • Free Library of Philadelphia, Parkway Central renovation/expansion — Modernized a 1927 landmark while preserving its Beaux-Arts character and expanding community programming.
      • Beinecke Rare Book and Manuscript Library renovation — Modernized a canonical mid-century special collections library without compromising its landmark architecture.
      • Library of Congress, Thomas Jefferson Building restoration — Sustains one of the most elaborately decorated public buildings in the United States.
      • Carnegie-era branch restorations (nationwide) — Preserves a shared national legacy of civic library architecture while meeting contemporary service needs.

      Key Findings

      • Andrew Carnegie funded 2,509 libraries worldwide between 1883 and 1929, including 1,689 in the United States, establishing a recognizable civic template (front steps, columns, 'FREE TO ALL') that shaped thousands of American towns.
      • U.S. library architecture moved through distinct eras — Beaux-Arts monuments (1890s-1910s), mid-century modernism (1950s-60s), Brutalism (1970s), Post-Modernism (1980s-90s), and the contemporary 'starchitecture' era (2000s-present) — each reflecting changing ideas about public knowledge and civic space.
      • The New York Public Library (Carrere & Hastings, 1911) and Boston Public Library McKim Building (McKim, Mead & White, 1895) are foundational Beaux-Arts models whose grand reading rooms set enduring standards for civic library grandeur.
      • Rem Koolhaas/OMA's Seattle Central Library (2004) redefined the central library through programmatic 'stacking' and the continuous books spiral, becoming the most influential contemporary library design.
      • Brutalist libraries such as Marcel Breuer's Atlanta-Fulton Central (1980, his final building) and Philip Johnson's BPL Johnson Building (1972) have sparked major preservation-versus-replacement debates, with the BPL renovation (2016, William Rawn) demonstrating how Brutalist buildings can be re-opened to the public.
      • Mid-century special-collections libraries remain architectural landmarks: Gordon Bunshaft/SOM's Beinecke Library (1963) with its translucent marble walls, William Pereira's Geisel Library (1970), and Louis Kahn's Phillips Exeter Library (1971, AIA Twenty-Five Year Award).
      • Moshe Safdie's Vancouver Public Library (1995) and Salt Lake City Main Library (2003) share a sweeping curved-glass civic form, illustrating how a single architect's vision can define a city's library identity.
      • The AIA/ALA Library Building Award is the premier U.S. recognition of library design excellence, jointly juried by architects and librarians; the NC State Hunt Library (Snøhetta, 2013) is a prominent recent winner.

      Library Copyright & Intellectual Property

      Copyright law shapes every aspect of library operations — from lending to digitization to interlibrary loan. This section covers the legal framework that governs how libraries manage copyrighted materials.

      Section 108 of the Copyright Act

      Controlled Digital Lending (CDL)

      Controlled Digital Lending (CDL) is a model in which a library digitizes a physical book it lawfully owns and lends the digital copy to one user at a time, maintaining an enforced one-to-one owned-to-loaned ratio. While a copy is lent out, the corresponding physical copy is made unavailable. Proponents argue CDL is supported by the first sale doctrine and fair use.

      Hachette v. Internet Archive:

      E-book Licensing vs. Ownership

      Unlike print books, which libraries own outright and may lend under the first sale doctrine, e-books are typically obtained under license agreements (end-user license agreements or terms of use) imposed by publishers and distributors. Licenses can override copyright's exceptions and impose terms, prices, and durations that have no analogue in the print world.

      Fair Use in Libraries

      • 1 — The purpose and character of the use, including whether it is commercial or nonprofit/educational and whether it is transformative (adding new meaning, message, or purpose).
      • 2 — The nature of the copyrighted work; factual/published works favor fair use more than creative/unpublished works.
      • 3 — The amount and substantiality of the portion used relative to the whole, including whether the 'heart' of the work was taken.
      • 4 — The effect of the use on the potential market for or value of the copyrighted work; uses that substitute for the original weigh heavily against fair use.

      Notable Copyright Cases

      CaseYearSignificance
      Williams & Wilkins Co. v. United States1973
      Bobbs-Merrill Co. v. Straus1908
      Basic Books, Inc. v. Kinko's Graphics Corp.1991
      American Geophysical Union v. Texaco, Inc.1994
      Princeton University Press v. Michigan Document Services, Inc.1996
      Cambridge University Press v. Becker (Georgia State University e-reserves case)2017
      Authors Guild v. HathiTrust2014
      Authors Guild v. Google, Inc. (Google Books case)2015
      Hachette Book Group, Inc. v. Internet Archive2024
      Kirtsaeng v. John Wiley & Sons, Inc.2013
      Capitol Records, LLC v. ReDigi Inc.2019
      Eldred v. Ashcroft2003

      Key Findings

      • U.S. copyright law provides libraries specific exceptions (Section 108, Section 107 fair use, Section 110/TEACH Act, Section 121) but they are narrow and fact-bound; the shift to digital has stressed traditional library practices.
      • The first sale doctrine underpins physical lending but does not robustly extend to e-books, which are licensed (not sold), DRM-protected, and governed by contract terms that can override copyright exceptions.
      • Publisher e-book licensing models (one-copy/one-user, simultaneous use, cost-per-circulation, metered access such as HarperCollins' 26-checkout cap) impose higher costs and shorter effective lifespans on library collections than print, straining materials budgets.
      • Controlled Digital Lending was rejected as fair use in Hachette v. Internet Archive: the S.D.N.Y. granted summary judgment on March 24, 2023, the Second Circuit affirmed on September 4, 2024, and the Internet Archive ended its appeal in December 2024, curtailing large-scale CDL of in-copyright works.
      • Fair use remains libraries' most flexible protection and has prevailed in key contexts: full-text search and print-disabled access (Authors Guild v. HathiTrust), mass digitization for search and snippets (Authors Guild v. Google), and many e-reserve uses (Cambridge University Press v. Becker).
      • Public Domain Day resumed in 2019 after a 20-year freeze caused by the 1998 CTEA; works from 1928, including Steamboat Willie, entered the public domain on January 1, 2024, and libraries are major digitizers and disseminators of these works.
      • Orphan works remain a significant, unresolved problem in U.S. law; despite U.S. Copyright Office recommendations in 2006 and 2015, no federal orphan works statute has been enacted.
      • DRM (Adobe ADEPT, OverDrive/Libby, Kindle, FairPlay) governs nearly all library digital lending, restricting copying, accessibility, preservation, and portability; DMCA Section 1201 limits circumvention, though triennial exemptions grant libraries limited preservation, accessibility, and research rights.

      Rural Libraries & Small Community Service

      Thousands of U.S. libraries serve communities of fewer than 10,000 people, often operating on minimal budgets with limited staff. This section details the unique challenges and innovations of rural library service.

      4000
      Estimated Systems Serving Under 10000
      IMLS Public Libraries Survey data consistently show roughly 4,000+ public library administrative entities serve Legal Service Area populations under 10,000. Depending on the survey year and the exact LSA threshold, estimates range from about 3,800 to 4,400 systems. ALA and IMLS both cite 'about 4,000' small and rural public libraries.

      Bookmobiles & Mobile Services

      767 bookmobiles operate nationwide. Bookmobile counts have declined steadily from a peak of over 1,000 in the late 20th century to 767 reported in the FY2024 survey. Declines reflect vehicle replacement costs, fuel prices, staffing constraints, and the expansion of fixed branches and digital services. Nonetheless bookmobiles remain essential for reaching geographically dispersed and homebound populations.

      Tribal Libraries

      Tribal libraries include tribal public libraries, tribal college libraries, and combined archive-library-museum cultural centers. The boundary between 'library,' 'archive,' and 'museum' is often blurred in Indian Country, where memory institutions frequently serve all three functions under one roof. ATALM was created precisely to serve this combined category of 'tribal archives, libraries, and museums.'

      Rural Broadband & Libraries

      {'finding': "In many rural communities the public library is the only source of free, public, high-speed internet access. Rural residents rely on library Wi-Fi and public computers for homework, job applications, telehealth, government services, and digital literacy help. The library's role as a broadband anchor institution is most acute where home broadband is unavailable or unaffordable.", 'documented_usage': 'IMLS Public Libraries Survey FY2024 recorded over 100 million public internet computer uses and over 418 million Wi-Fi sessions nationally (pls_extended_metrics.json). In rural areas the per-capita reliance on these services is disproportionately high.', 'source': 'IMLS Public Libraries Survey FY2024 (pls_extended_metrics.json); ALA Digital Inclusion surveys'}

      Rural Library Success Stories

      • Anythink Libraries (Rangeview Library District, Adams County, Colorado) — Anythink transformed a struggling rural-suburban library district into a nationally recognized innovator with themed branches (e.g., Anythink Wright 'The Studio,' Anythink Huron 'The Bakehouse'), maker programming, and a brand built on imagination. While Adams County spans suburban and rural areas, Anythink's model demonstrates how small-community library systems can lead nationally. Anythink has won multiple library design and innovation awards.
      • Nelson Memorial Library / Nelson County, Virginia — Rural Virginia library systems serving Nelson County exemplify how small rural libraries anchor civic and cultural life in mountain communities. Nelson County's library services demonstrate rural innovation in programming, local history, and community resilience, including recovery-oriented services following natural disasters.
      • Scandinavia Public Library (Wisconsin) — Often cited as one of the smallest public libraries in the United States, the Scandinavia Public Library serves the tiny Waupaca County village of Scandinavia, Wisconsin (population a few hundred). It is frequently referenced in ALA and library-press accounts as an exemplar of how the smallest libraries sustain community access.
      • Pottsboro Area Public Library (Texas) — A small rural library on Lake Texoma that gained national attention for innovative community engagement and for pioneering approaches to funding and services in a small Texas town. Frequently cited in rural library innovation discussions.
      • Crawford County Public Library / small Indiana rural libraries — Rural Indiana county libraries demonstrate how small systems deliver broadband, workforce, and early-literacy services across dispersed populations with minimal staff.

      Rural Library Challenges

      • Overview: Rural libraries face a convergent set of structural challenges: tiny tax bases, severe staffing shortages, limited hours, small collections, technology gaps, and long distances to the nearest library. These challenges compound: a single staff member cannot both run the desk and pursue grants, and a small collection cannot satisfy diverse community needs without robust interlibrary loan - which itself depends on the very broadband and staffing that are scarce.
      • Source: IMLS Public Libraries Survey; ALA; harvested public_libraries.csv and supporting summaries

      Library Accessibility & ADA Compliance

      Libraries are required by the Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA) to provide equal access to their services, programs, and facilities. This section details the scope of library accessibility services — from physical accommodations to assistive technology to the National Library Service for the Blind and Print Disabled.

      National Library Service for the Blind and Print Disabled (NLS)

      The Library of Congress National Library Service for the Blind and Print Disabled (NLS) is the primary U.S. federal program providing free braille and talking-book library service to people who are blind or have visual, physical, or reading disabilities. Founded by the Pratt-Smoot Act of 1931, NLS operates through a nationwide cooperating network and delivers materials by postage-free mail and via the BARD digital download service. Complementary providers (Bookshare, Learning Ally, the American Printing House for the Blind) and local library braille/large-print collections extend access.

      219589
      Registered Patrons
      219,589 total readers served in FY2024
      Registered Patrons Text
      NLS historically served 500,000+ registered patrons cumulatively; the FY2024 annual active-reader count was 219,589, and public reporting commonly cites the program as serving approximately half a million eligible readers nationwide.
      Registered Patrons Note
      22188638
      Items Circulated Annually
      more than 22.2 million copies of braille, audio, and large print items circulated in FY2024
      Items Circulated Text
      more than 24.5 million copies (FY2023, per LOC Year at a Glance)
      Items Circulated Fy2023 Text

      Common ADA Accommodations

      • Qualified sign language interpreters for programs (on request)
      • Real-time captioning (CART) for events
      • Large-print and braille materials; screen-reader-compatible documents
      • Assistive listening devices / hearing loops
      • Accessible seating and reserved spaces at programs
      • Modified program formats (sensory-friendly hours, relaxed rules)
      • Homebound / books-by-mail delivery
      • Flexible library card policies and assistance with accommodations
      • Materials retrieval from out-of-reach shelving by staff
      • Alternative-format versions of library-produced documents and forms

      Sensory-Friendly Programming

      Sensory-friendly and autism-friendly programming is a growing area of inclusive library service, addressing the needs of autistic and neurodivergent patrons and their families. Common offerings include sensory-friendly hours, quiet spaces and sensory rooms, adapted storytimes with predictable structure, and fidget/sensory tools. There is no single authoritative national count of libraries offering sensory-friendly programming.

      Cognitive Accessibility

      Libraries serve patrons with cognitive and intellectual disabilities and people living with dementia through simplified/easy-read collections, inclusive programming, memory cafes and dementia-friendly services, plain-language materials and signage, and partnerships with disability and aging organizations. These services overlap with print-disability eligibility (reading disabilities qualify for NLS) and with senior services.

      • Next Chapter Book Club

      Accessibility Funding

      Accessibility in U.S. libraries is funded through a layered mix of federal grants (IMLS Library Services and Technology Act Grants to States, National Leadership Grants, Laura Bush 21st Century Librarian Program, and American Rescue Plan grants), the Library of Congress NLS appropriation, state library agency budgets, local library operating funds, and targeted programs (Bookshare/OSEP, Native American Library Services). Capital accessibility improvements (automatic doors, ramps, accessible restrooms, hearing loops) are typically financed through local capital budgets and occasionally IMLS or state grants.

      Key Findings

      • The Americans with Disabilities Act of 1990 applies to public libraries under Title II (state/local government) and Title III (public accommodations), requiring effective communication through auxiliary aids/services and compliance with the 2010 ADA Standards for Accessible Design; no single national database publishes ADA compliance rates for all U.S. public libraries.
      • In April 2024, the DOJ issued a final rule under Title II requiring state and local government web and mobile app content (including public library sites) to conform to WCAG 2.1 Level AA, with compliance deadlines of April 24, 2026 (large entities) and April 26, 2027 (small entities).
      • The Library of Congress NLS, founded by the Pratt-Smoot Act of 1931, is the flagship federal program for blind and print-disabled readers; in FY2024 it circulated more than 22.2 million copies to 219,589 active readers (commonly cited as serving ~500,000 eligible patrons) with a collection of 332,077 titles on a $66.13 million appropriation.
      • NLS operates through a nationwide network of 101 cooperating libraries (55 regional, 32 subregional, and 14 advisory/outreach centers) serving every U.S. state and territory, grown from 19 libraries in 1931; BARD recorded 4,257,667 audio downloads for 46,646 patrons in FY2024.
      • NLS completed the nationwide rollout of its Braille eReader program in FY2024, deploying approximately 8,500 refreshable braille display devices; under the Marrakesh Treaty it acquired 1,987 works in 8 languages that year, and the WIPO Accessible Books Consortium holds 202,771 NLS-produced titles.
      • Physical accessibility is governed by the 2010 ADA Standards (ramps at 1:12 max slope, accessible parking/restrooms, reach ranges of 15-48 inches, accessible service counters); FY2024 capital expenditures of $2.10 billion were reported by only 41.3% of systems (3,823 of 9,249), the principal local funding source for accessibility renovations, while 767 bookmobiles extended service to patrons unable to visit fixed branches.
      • Under the ADA, libraries must provide qualified sign language interpreters (ASL, oral, cued-speech, tactile) and captioning for deaf/hard-of-hearing patrons; NIDCD estimates roughly 25 million U.S. adults have some hearing loss, and NLS loans a high-volume talking-book machine amplified to 120 dB at no cost.
      • Autism prevalence among U.S. children is 1 in 36 (CDC ADDM 2023, based on 2020 data); sensory-friendly hours, sensory rooms, and autism-friendly storytimes are a growing but uneven area of inclusive service with no national count, while roughly 6.7 million Americans aged 65+ live with Alzheimer's dementia, driving dementia-friendly memory cafes and reminiscence kits.
      • Assistive technology spans commercial screen readers (JAWS, ZoomText), free/open-source and built-in tools (NVDA, VoiceOver, TalkBack, Narrator), video magnifiers/CCTV, text-to-speech/OCR, and refreshable braille displays; adaptive computer workstations are common at large/NLS-affiliated libraries but uneven at small/rural systems due to cost and IT-support constraints.
      • ADA enforcement against libraries proceeds through DOJ complaints and mediation, Project Civic Access city/county-wide settlements (which routinely include public libraries), Section 504 complaints, and private Title II/III lawsuits clustered around physical/program access, effective communication, and digital/website accessibility; the 2024 DOJ web rule responds to persistent non-compliance, with ~96% of top websites showing detectable WCAG failures (WebAIM Million 2024).

      Libraries & Food/Nutrition Security

      A rapidly growing area of library community service: libraries across America are addressing food insecurity through summer meal programs, food pantries, seed libraries, community gardens, and nutrition education.

      Summer Meal Programs

      The USDA Summer Food Service Program (SFSP) reimburses providers who serve free meals to children and teens in low-income areas when school is not in session. Public libraries have become an increasingly important SFSP site type because they are open throughout the summer, located in neighborhoods, carry no stigma, and can pair meals with their established summer reading programs. When children eat lunch at the library, they are also drawn into reading and learning activities, addressing both summer hunger and summer learning loss at the same time.

      Seed Libraries

      Seed libraries are community collections of vegetable, herb, and flower seeds that patrons can borrow for free, plant at home, grow to maturity, save seeds from the harvest, and return some of those seeds to the library for the next grower. Hosted overwhelmingly in public libraries, the seed library movement has grown from a single library in 2010 to an estimated 500+ seed libraries across North America and beyond, making it one of the fastest-growing food-related library movements.

      Cooking & Nutrition Literacy

      Libraries increasingly offer cooking classes, nutrition workshops, and food-literacy programming. The most ambitious of these are dedicated teaching kitchens built inside libraries, which allow hands-on cooking instruction. These programs treat cooking and nutrition as core literacy skills alongside reading and digital literacy, recognizing that the ability to prepare healthy food from basic ingredients is essential to nutrition security.

      Notable Food Programs at Libraries

      LibraryCityStateProgram
      Free Library of Philadelphia - Culinary Literacy CenterPhiladelphiaPennsylvaniaOpened in 2014 at the Parkway Central Library, the Culinary Literacy Center is a dedicated teaching kitchen offering hands-on cooking classes, chef demonstrations, and nutrition education that integrates cooking with reading, math, and science. Considered the first library teaching kitchen in the U.S. and a national model.
      Richmond Grows Seed Lending LibraryRichmondCaliforniaFounded in 2010 at the Richmond Public Library and credited as the first seed library in a public library. Its freely shared open-source model catalyzed a national seed library movement now estimated at 500+ libraries.
      California Lunch at the Library (statewide)Multiple (statewide)CaliforniaA California State Library, California Library Association, and California Summer Meal Coalition initiative that grew to 200+ library summer meal sites serving free USDA SFSP lunches alongside summer reading.       Cuyahoga County Public LibraryParma (Cleveland area)OhioSeveral branches partner with the Greater Cleveland Food Bank to host recurring mobile food pantry distributions in branch parking lots, serving hundreds of households per distribution.
      San Antonio Public LibrarySan AntonioTexasOffers food access and nutrition programming at branches serving high-need neighborhoods, including partnerships that connect patrons to SNAP enrollment and fresh food resources.

      Key Findings

      • {'finding': 'Libraries have become essential food security infrastructure. Combining summer meals, food pantries, seed libraries, gardens, cooking classes, SNAP/WIC enrollment, and farmers markets, some libraries now function as neighborhood food hubs - often the most accessible food resource in their community, especially in food deserts.'}
      • {'finding': "The summer meal model pairs food with literacy. Library summer meal programs (exemplified by California's Lunch at the Library, with 200+ sites) uniquely address summer hunger and summer learning loss simultaneously, drawing meal participants into reading programs and vice versa."}
      • {'finding': 'Seed libraries are a fast-growing, homegrown movement. From one library (Richmond Grows, 2010) the model expanded to an estimated 500+ seed libraries, returning the means of food production to communities and rebuilding seed-saving knowledge lost to industrial agriculture.'}
      • {'finding': 'Partnerships are essential. Libraries rarely operate food programs alone. Food banks (Feeding America network), USDA, Cooperative Extension, SNAP-Ed, and state library agencies supply the food, funding, expertise, and reimbursement capacity that libraries lack in-house.'}
      • {'finding': "Trust and non-stigma are the library's competitive advantage. Because libraries serve everyone regardless of income, patrons seeking food help there face less stigma than at a food bank, improving reach to people who would otherwise go without help."}
      • {'finding': "The COVID-19 pandemic accelerated and normalized library food distribution. Contactless parking-lot and curbside distributions during 2020-2021 proved the library's operational capacity as a food site, and many of these distributions became permanent."}
      • {'finding': "Teaching kitchens elevate cooking to a literacy. The Free Library of Philadelphia's Culinary Literacy Center (2014) established the model of a dedicated library teaching kitchen, reframing cooking and nutrition as core literacy skills and inspiring similar facilities elsewhere."}
      • {'finding': 'Seed law had to catch up with seed libraries. The 2014 Pennsylvania case and the subsequent American Association of Seed Control Officials resolution clarified that noncommercial community seed sharing should be accommodated under state seed law, securing the legal footing the movement needed to grow.'}

      Federal Depository Library Program (FDLP)

      The Federal Depository Library Program, administered by the U.S. Government Publishing Office (GPO), ensures that the American public has free access to U.S. government information. Established by Congress in 1813, the FDLP distributes government documents to designated libraries nationwide — from the earliest depository designated in 1814 to the newest in 2025.

      National Library of Medicine (NLM)

      The National Library of Medicine (NLM), operated by the United States federal government, is the world's largest biomedical library. Located on the campus of the National Institutes of Health in Bethesda, Maryland, NLM has been a center of information innovation since its founding in 1836. It maintains and makes available a vast print collection and produces electronic information services on a wide range of topics that are searched billions of times each year by millions of people around the globe. NLM also supports and conducts research, development, and training in biomedical informatics and health information technology, and coordinates an 8,000+ member Network of the National Library of Medicine that promotes and provides access to health information in communities across the United States.
