      Key Databases & Resources

      DatabaseDescription
      PubMedA free database primarily including the MEDLINE database of references and abstracts on life sciences and biomedical topics. Maintained by NLM at NIH as part of the Entrez system of information retrieval. First released January 1996; offered free to the public starting June 1997.
      MEDLINEMedical Literature Analysis and Retrieval System Online. A bibliographic database of life sciences and biomedical information covering medicine, nursing, pharmacy, dentistry, veterinary medicine, and health care. The online version of MEDLARS, launched in 1971; compiled by NLM and freely searchable via PubMed.
      PubMed Central (PMC)A free digital repository that archives openly accessible full-text scholarly articles published in biomedical and life sciences journals. More than a document repository - submissions are indexed and formatted for enhanced metadata, medical ontology, and unique identifiers. A major research database developed by NCBI.
      ClinicalTrials.govA registry of clinical trials run by NLM at NIH. Launched February 29, 2000 following the Food and Drug Administration Modernization Act of 1997. Expanded by FDA Amendments Act of 2007 to require registration and results reporting.
      National Center for Biotechnology Information (NCBI)An intramural division within NLM that creates public databases in molecular biology, conducts research in computational biology, develops software tools for analyzing molecular and genomic data, and disseminates biomedical information. Founded November 4, 1988 through legislation sponsored by Congressman Claude Pepper.
      GenBankAn open access, annotated collection of publicly available nucleotide sequences and their protein translations. Part of the International Nucleotide Sequence Database Collaboration (INSDC). Produced and maintained by NCBI at NLM/NIH. Established in 1982 by Walter Goad at Los Alamos National Laboratory.
      MedlinePlusAn online consumer health information service produced by NLM. Provides curated consumer health information in English and Spanish with select content in additional languages. Brings together information from NLM, NIH, other U.S. government agencies, and health-related organizations. Introduced October 1998.
      MeSH (Medical Subject Headings)The NLM controlled vocabulary thesaurus used for indexing articles for PubMed/MEDLINE. A comprehensive life science vocabulary that enables consistent retrieval of biomedical literature.
      ChemIDplusA chemical database of over 400,000 chemicals complete with names, synonyms, and structures. Includes links to NLM and other databases and resources, including federal, state, and international agencies.
      LocatorPlusNLM's online catalog, providing access to the library's collection holdings.
      TOXNET (Toxicology Data Network)Toxicology and environmental health information system established at NLM in 1967. Includes databases compiled from medical literature and governmental/nongovernmental organization files for chemical emergency response and public education.
      Unified Medical Language System (UMLS)A set of files and software that brings together many health and biomedical vocabularies and standards to enable interoperability between computer systems. NLM is the U.S. National Release Center for SNOMED CT.

      Network of the National Library of Medicine (NNLM)

      NNLM is coordinated by 14 institutions across the United States. Member organizations provide health professionals and the general public with health information resources and services. The network promotes and provides access to health information in communities across the United States.

      Key Facts

      • World's largest biomedical library, founded in 1836 as a small collection of medical books in the office of the U.S. Army Surgeon General
      • Located on the campus of the National Institutes of Health in Bethesda, Maryland, since 1962
      • An institute within the National Institutes of Health (NIH), part of the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services
      • Collection of 27.8 million items (2015), including 7+ million books, journals, technical reports, manuscripts, microfilms, photographs, and images
      • Annual budget of US$341,119,000 (FY2016 appropriation)
      • Employs approximately 1,741 staff
      • Electronic information resources searched billions of times each year by millions of people around the globe
      • Coordinates the Network of the National Library of Medicine (NNLM) with 8,000+ member organizations across 7 regions
      • PubMed contains 40+ million citations and abstracts (as of March 2025)
      • PubMed Central archives 10.8 million full-text biomedical and life sciences articles

      Prison Libraries & Incarcerated Reader Services

      Library services to incarcerated people represent a critical but often overlooked dimension of American librarianship. The American Library Association has advocated for prison library services since 1911, yet access remains inconsistent across the 50 states.

      Pell Grant For Incarcerated Reinstated
      The 1994 ban on federal Pell Grant eligibility for incarcerated people ended on July 1, 2023, restoring access to federal financial aid for higher education in prison programs - a parallel development to the expansion of prison library services.
      Pell Grant Context

      Library Access in Prisons

      • Official Access Rate Note: No federal statistical agency publishes the percentage of U.S. prisons with library services. BJS collects prison and prisoner counts but not library-access data; BOP does not publish a public inventory of facility libraries; state departments of corrections report inconsistently. The figures below are qualitative findings and informal estimates, not official statistics.
      • Ala Finding On Service Quality: ALA (2025) reports that many so-called prison libraries are 'little more than a few dusty shelves, often not managed by trained library workers.' Some prisons have libraries 'worthy of the name,' but many others have 'only minimal or nonexistent services,' meaning many incarcerated people have 'no real access to a meaningful library and the expertise of librarians.'
      • Ithaka Finding On Law Vs Recreational: Ithaka S+R (2024) found that in some facilities the law library is effectively the only library resource, because departments of corrections are generally obligated to provide legal-access resources, while recreational reading libraries are 'effectively non-existent.'
      • Ala Adequate Funding Per Capita Usd: 13
      • Filter Estimated Facilities Act Could Fund: 500
      • Filter Estimated Share Of State Prisons: about one-third
      • Filter Estimate Basis: Using ALA's $13/capita adequate-funding benchmark against the $60M over six years, the Act might fund services at roughly 500 facilities - about one-third of state prisons. This is an informal journalist estimate, not an official projection.
      • Prison Library Staffing Range: Ithaka S+R (2024): staffing ranges from a professional librarian to a correctional officer to incarcerated individuals themselves. Many prison libraries depend on donations and outside organizations (including public libraries) for books and resources.
      • Training Gap: Ithaka S+R (2024): none of the interviewed librarians had received specific training in prison librarianship; it is usually treated as a 'special library' topic in library schools with few training opportunities.
      • Covid Impact: Ithaka S+R (2024): during COVID-19 many prison libraries were closed completely or sectioned off for social distancing, and prison libraries were impacted the most when departments of corrections made budget and staffing cuts.

      Library Programs & Community Services

      Beyond books, U.S. libraries deliver an extraordinary range of programs serving every age group — from summer reading that reaches millions of children to adult literacy, STEM education, author events, health services, and outreach to those who cannot visit the building.

      tens of millions (approximate)
      Annual summer reading registrants
      2
      National summer reading programs
      1987
      CSLP founded

      Summer Reading Programs

      Summer reading is the single most universal public-library program in the United States. Two national collaboratives supply the artwork, manuals, incentives, and slogans that the majority of public libraries use: the Collaborative Summer Library Program (CSLP), a grassroots consortium, and iREAD, a program of the Illinois Library Association (ILA). Summer reading's documented purpose is to prevent the 'summer slide' in reading achievement, especially among children from low-income households who lack access to books when school is not in session.

      Collaborative Summer Library Program (CSLP) — Founded 1987. A member-funded consortium. Member states/territories pay dues and, through shared resources and collaborative committees, make it easier and more affordable for libraries to host high-quality summer reading programs. Any profit from the sale of materials is reinvested into the organization.

      iREAD (Illinois Library Association) — Supplies high-quality, low-cost reading-program resources and products (artwork, manuals, incentives) to libraries by purchase. Originally an Illinois program ('I Read') that expanded nationally; libraries outside Illinois may buy iREAD materials.

      Impact Studies

      • The Dominican Study: Public Library Summer Reading Programs Close the Reading Gap (, 2010): Students who participated in public-library summer reading programs scored higher on reading-achievement tests than non-participants and did not experience the 'summer slide'; the effect was strongest for students in the lowest socioeconomic groups. The study is the most widely cited evidence base for the academic value of library summer reading.
      • Reading Is Fundamental (RIF) / library access research: Children's access to self-selected books over the summer is associated with measurable gains in reading proficiency; library summer-reading programs are a principal mechanism for delivering that access at scale.

      Early Literacy Programs

      Early literacy -- the pre-reading skills children develop from birth to age five -- is a core mission of U.S. public libraries. Programming centers on storytimes and on educating parents and caregivers to be their child's first teacher. The dominant national framework is Every Child Ready to Read (ECRR), complemented by grassroots challenges such as 1000 Books Before Kindergarten and 'Born to Read' newborn programs.

      Every Child Ready to Read (ECRR) @ your library — A parent-education initiative. PLA and ALSC concluded that public libraries could have an even greater impact on early literacy by educating parents and caregivers (rather than only modeling skills in storytimes). Teaching caregivers how to support early-literacy development multiplies the effect of library efforts (ECRR site).

      1000 Books Before Kindergarten — A nationwide reading challenge: read a book (any book, repeats count) to a newborn, infant, and/or toddler, with the goal of 1,000 books before the child starts kindergarten. The 1000 Books Foundation enables libraries, schools, churches, and other nonprofit entities to host the challenge locally; contributions are tax-deductible (1000booksbeforekindergarten.org).

      Storytime Program Types

      • Baby storytime (0-18 months)
      • Toddler storytime (18-36 months)
      • Preschool storytime (3-5 years)
      • Family / bilingual storytime
      • Sensory-friendly storytime

      Adult Education & Lifelong Learning

      Public libraries are among the most accessible adult-education sites in the country, often partnering with adult-education providers, workforce boards, USCIS, and the National Network of Libraries of Medicine. Programming spans basic literacy, English for Speakers of Other Languages (ESOL), high-school equivalency (GED) preparation, citizenship preparation, digital literacy, and job/career services.

      Program AreaDescription
      GED & Adult LiteracyMany public libraries host GED/HSE (high-school equivalency) classes or study groups, often in partnership with local adult education consortia, community colleges, or state literacy organizations. Libraries also provide quiet study space, test-prep books, and online-learning access for self-study.
      ESL/ESOLLibraries are major providers of ESOL conversation groups and classes, frequently staffed by volunteers. Classes are typically free and offered in multiple skill levels; large urban systems serve thousands of adult learners annually.
      Citizenship ClassesLibraries partner with U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services (USCIS) to host Citizenship Corners and naturalization information sessions. IMLS has funded numerous citizenship projects. Libraries offer civics/civics-test study materials, interview-prep classes, and referrals to Board of Immigration Appeals (BIA)-accredited representatives.
      Job Training & WorkforceLibraries offer resume help, interview coaching, job-search assistance, and workforce partnerships. Many partner with American Job Centers / state workforce boards under the Workforce Innovation and Opportunity Act (WIOA). The Public Library Association's Libraries Transforming Communities and workforce initiatives document this role.
      Digital LiteracyPublic libraries are frontline providers of digital-skills training. The PLA's DigitalLearn.org offers free, self-paced modules; Literacy Minnesota's Northstar Digital Literacy Assessment (since 2012) is widely adopted by libraries to assess and certify basic computer skills. Topics include email, internet basics, word processing, and online safety.

      STEM & STEAM Programming

      Libraries have become significant informal STEM/STEAM learning spaces, especially for children and teens who lack access to school-based or fee-based enrichment. Makerspaces, coding clubs, robotics, 3D printing, and federally funded initiatives such as NASA@ My Library expand the library's role beyond reading.

      NASA@ My Library — Funder: NASA Science Mission Directorate (SMD). A national STEM initiative that provides public libraries with Earth and space science resources, STEM activity kits, and training. STAR Net also delivers traveling exhibitions ('Discover Earth,' 'Discover Space,' 'Discover Health,' 'Discover Technology'). Current offerings include a Heliophysics Workshop Series in collaboration with NASA SCoPE, plus 'Learn to Code' tutorials and 'Make Your Library's Case for STEM' resources (starnetlibraries.org).

      STAR Net traveling STEM exhibitions —

      • Discover Earth
      • Discover Space
      • Discover Health
      • Discover Technology

      Coding & Robotics: Libraries run coding clubs, robotics leagues, and girls-in-STEM programs, often as affiliates of national organizations.

      • Girls Who Code — Free after-school coding clubs hosted at libraries
      • FIRST / LEGO robotics — Robotics teams and robotics-themed summer camps
      • Hour of Code / Code.org — Annual computer-science participation events
      • CS First (Google) — Block-based coding clubs for youth
      • Black Girls CODE / Black Code Camps — Community coding events, sometimes library-hosted

      Book Clubs & Reading Groups

      Libraries are the most common free host of book discussion groups in the United States, ranging from informal staff-led clubs to large, community-wide reading programs backed by federal grants. Community-wide reads were popularized in the late 1990s and have become a signature library program.

      ProgramDetails
      One Book, One CommunityA whole community reads the same title and attends discussions, author talks, and related events over weeks or months.
      Great DecisionsAmerica's largest grassroots discussion program on world affairs. Libraries host Great Decisions discussion groups using the annual Great Decisions briefing book and accompanying PBS documentary series. Participants often register opinions via the FPA National Opinion Ballot.
      NEA Big ReadA community-grant program in which organizations (often public libraries) receive grants to run a month-long series of events around a single selected book. Goals: inspire meaningful conversations, celebrate local creativity, elevate diverse voices, and build community connections (arts.gov).
      Oprah Book Club InfluenceLaunched on 'The Oprah Winfrey Show' in 1996, Oprah's Book Club dramatically boosted sales and library holds for selected titles and helped normalize celebrity/curator book clubs. Libraries responded by purchasing additional copies and building waiting lists, and many launched 'read-alike' discussion series.

      Author Events & Book Festivals

      Author talks and book festivals are a marquee public-service offering of U.S. libraries. The flagship national event is the Library of Congress National Book Festival; locally, nearly every public library system hosts author talks, often free of charge, frequently in partnership with Friends groups, publishers, and regional book festivals.

      Library of Congress National Book Festival — Organized by Library of Congress (LOC), launched 2001. First held September 8, 2001; launched by then-First Lady Laura Bush, a former teacher and librarian. The festival was held outdoors on the National Mall for many years before moving indoors.

      Public libraries are core partners in the U.S. book-festival circuit, often co-presenting events or serving as venues and booksellers. Notable partner festivals include state and city festivals across the country.

      • Library of Congress National Book Festival — Washington, DC
      • Los Angeles Times Festival of Books — Los Angeles, CA
      • Texas Book Festival — Austin, TX
      • Brooklyn Book Festival — Brooklyn, NY
      • Printers Row Lit Fest (Chicago) — Chicago, IL
      • Miami Book Fair — Miami, FL
      • Wordstock / Portland Book Festival — Portland, OR

      Health & Wellness Services

      Libraries increasingly serve as community health-and-wellness hubs, extending their public-service mission into trauma-informed care, mental-health support, health literacy, and even clinical services such as vaccination. The public-health emergencies of the 2010s and the COVID-19 pandemic accelerated this role.

      Service AreaDescription
      Trauma-Informed ServicesA growing body of practice frames libraries as trauma-informed spaces: staff training on trauma and resilience, environmental design for safety and calm, and partnerships with behavioral-health providers. The Free Library of Philadelphia has been a nationally recognized leader.
      Social Workers in LibrariesA growing number of urban public libraries embed licensed social workers or peer navigators to connect patrons experiencing homelessness, mental-health crises, or substance use with services.
      Mental Health ResourcesLibraries provide mental-health information, host support groups and talks, and increasingly partner with organizations like the National Alliance on Mental Illness (NAMI). Some distribute free naloxone (Narcan) and train staff in overdose response.
      Health Literacy
      Wellness ProgrammingLibraries host free wellness classes as part of community health programming.
      Vaccine & Clinical ServicesDuring the COVID-19 pandemic, many public libraries served as vaccination sites in partnership with state and local health departments and the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA). Libraries also routinely host seasonal flu-shot clinics and health-screening events (blood pressure, vision, dental).

      Cultural & Arts Programming

      Cultural and arts programming is a long-standing library function. Libraries mount exhibitions, host concerts and film series, lead craft workshops, and program around national cultural-heritage months, often as the only free arts venue in a community.

      Program TypeDetails
      Art ExhibitionsMany libraries operate gallery space for rotating exhibitions by local artists and traveling exhibitions. ALA has administered Smithsonian and NEH-funded traveling exhibitions for libraries (e.g., the 'Museum on Main Street' partnership and ALA's traveling-exhibition program). Examples: Rotating community-art gallery walls; ALA/Smithsonian traveling exhibitions; NEH-funded traveling exhibitions (see data/neh_library_grants_summary.json); Youth art shows and county-fair partnerships
      Concerts & Performing ArtsFree concert series and performances are common, often in partnership with local arts councils and Friends groups. Examples: Summer concert series on library grounds; Classical/jazz recitals indoors; Children's performers and puppeteers; Spoken-word and poetry slams
      Film ScreeningsLibraries host film screenings and discussion series. Public performance licensing (e.g., via Movie Licensing USA / Swank Motion Pictures, or Criterion Pictures) enables libraries to show copyrighted films to audiences.
      Craft WorkshopsHands-on creative workshops for all ages, frequently tied to makerspace equipment or seasonal themes. Examples: Knitting/crochet circles; Painting and drawing classes; Scrapbooking and journaling; STEM/craft hybrids (e.g., e-textiles, 3D design); Holiday and cultural craft programs
      Cultural Heritage MonthsLibraries routinely develop exhibits, booklists, and programs to mark federally recognized heritage months, supporting inclusive collections and community visibility.

      Outreach Services

      Outreach extends library service beyond the building to people who cannot easily visit: bookmobiles, homebound delivery, correctional facilities, hospitals, and daycare/Head Start centers. Outreach is tracked in the IMLS Public Libraries Survey and is a core equity function.

      767
      Bookmobiles nationwide
      Outreach TypeDescription
      BookmobilesMobile library vehicles that bring collections and programming to rural stops, schools, senior facilities, and underserved neighborhoods. The first U.S. bookmobile is widely credited to the Washington County Free Library (Hagerstown, Maryland), which launched a horse-drawn book wagon in 1905.
      Homebound ServicesDelivery of materials to patrons who are homebound due to age, disability, or illness. Often staffed by volunteers or dedicated outreach staff; materials are selected to patron preference (large print, audiobooks, DVDs). Complements the Library of Congress National Library Service for the Blind and Print Disabled (NLS).
      Prison Library ServicesLibraries provide services to people who are incarcerated through state library agency programs (e.g., 'Library Services to the Institutionalized' IMLS grants), jail libraries, reentry book-discussion programs, and book donations. Public libraries also serve as reentry hubs (ID assistance, job help, expungement clinics).
      Hospital LibrariesPatient libraries serve hospitals (including children's hospitals) and long-term-care facilities, providing bedside reading, activity carts, and consumer-health information. Some are operated by health sciences libraries or volunteer auxiliaries.
      Daycare & Head Start VisitsYouth-services librarians conduct outreach storytimes at daycare centers, Head Start classrooms, and preschools, modeling ECRR Five Practices for caregivers and distributing library-card information. This is a primary early-literacy equity strategy for children not yet able to visit the library.

      Key Findings

      • U.S. public libraries hosted approximately 5.09 million programs in FY2024 with about 105 million in attendance across 9,249 systems (IMLS PLS); children's programs (ages 0-11) account for the largest share, drawing ~59.7M of the ~105M attendance.
      • IMLS PLS FY2022 recorded 5,085,068 programs and 105,321,602 attendance, split into 2.45M children's, 510K young-adult, 1.66M adult, and 4.32M online programs (avg 20.7 attendees per program); online programs remain a permanent post-COVID modality.
      • Summer reading is the most universal U.S. library program, supplied nationally by two collaboratives: CSLP (founded 1987 by ten Minnesota library systems) and iREAD (Illinois Library Association).
      • The Dominican Study (Roman & Celano, 2010, IMLS/ALA) found public-library summer reading closes the summer reading slide, with the strongest effect for low-income students.
      • Every Child Ready to Read (PLA + ALSC) reframed early literacy around parent education and the Five Practices (talk, sing, read, write, play); its 2nd edition was developed by a 2008 joint ALA/PLA task force.
      • 1000 Books Before Kindergarten, run by the 1000 Books Foundation (a 501(c)(3)), is hosted by thousands of libraries and sponsors an annual one-day #TheBigRead.
      • The NEA Big Read (since 2006) funds community-wide reads; the 2026-2027 cycle theme is 'America250' with a 24-title NEA Big Read Library tied to the U.S. Semiquincentennial.
      • The Library of Congress National Book Festival (since 2001, launched by Laura Bush) is the flagship national author event; the 2026 festival is August 22 at the Walter E. Washington Convention Center.
      • NASA@ My Library (Space Science Institute / STAR Net + ALA, funded by NASA) and STAR Net traveling exhibitions are the principal federally backed library STEM initiatives.
      • The Network of the National Library of Medicine (NNLM, established 1965) drives library health literacy, including the Consumer Health Information Specialization (CHIS) for librarians.

      Special Collections & Rare Books

      America's libraries hold some of the world's most extraordinary special collections — from Gutenberg Bibles to presidential archives to rare manuscripts worth millions.

      Key Findings

      • Special collections are defined less by subject than by rarity, uniqueness, fragility, and research value; they are non-circulating, paged, and served under supervised, environmentally controlled conditions distinct from general library stacks.
      • The United States hosts an exceptional concentration of rare-book and manuscript repositories, ranging from university libraries (Beinecke, Houghton, Bancroft, Harry Ransom Center) to independent research institutions (Newberry, Huntington, Morgan, Folger, American Philosophical Society).
      • The most iconic printed treasures are tightly concentrated: approximately 49 Gutenberg Bibles survive worldwide, held by a small number of institutions including the Library of Congress, Yale, UT Austin, the Morgan Library, and Princeton; the Folger Shakespeare Library alone holds 82 of the 235 known First Folios.
      • The NARA presidential library system comprises thirteen libraries covering administrations from Herbert Hoover through George W. Bush, with the Barack Obama Presidential Library under development under a modified model, together forming the national record of the modern presidency.
      • Mass digitization - Google Books, HathiTrust (>17 million volumes), the Internet Archive, and the DPLA - has transformed discovery and access, while IIIF and Omeka provide the interoperable infrastructure for scholarly digital exhibits of special-collections content.
      • Preservation depends most on stable environmental controls (temperature, humidity, light, pest management) and written disaster plans; the IMLS Connecting to Collections initiative (2007 onward) significantly raised national preservation capacity, especially for smaller institutions.
      • Access is deliberately constrained: reading-room rules, registration, restricted materials, and reproduction limits balance scholarly use against preservation and rights obligations, with archival copyright frequently complicated by orphan works and donor agreements.
      • Rare-book theft remains a serious threat: the Blumberg case (roughly 23,600 stolen books from about 327 institutions) and the Smiley map thefts demonstrate the need for supervised reading rooms, strict inventory controls, and profession-wide security guidelines.

      Library Privacy & Surveillance

      Protecting patron privacy is a core value of librarianship. This section covers the legal framework, surveillance threats, and privacy-protective practices that safeguard what people read and access.

      Key Findings

      • Privacy is a core professional value of librarianship: the ALA Code of Ethics (Article III), the Library Bill of Rights, and the Privacy Interpretation together make the confidentiality of users' reading and browsing records an ethical duty binding on librarians -- not merely a policy preference.
      • Every U.S. state and the District of Columbia has some legal protection for library user records, but the form varies: dedicated confidentiality statutes (e.g., New York CPLR 4509, Colorado 24-90-119, Illinois 75 ILCS 70/1, Michigan MCL 397.603, Connecticut Gen. Stat. 11-25), open-records exemptions (e.g., Washington RCW 42.56.310, California Gov't Code 6254(j)), or hybrid regimes. Penalties range from misdemeanors to contempt to no library-specific penalty.
      • The FBI Library Awareness Program (internally 'Development of Counterintelligence Among Librarians,' active from as early as 1973 and publicly disclosed September 18, 1987) was the formative event of modern library privacy advocacy; it prompted the 1988 congressional hearings, the adoption of library FBI-visit policies, and the enactment of state confidentiality statutes such as New York's CPLR 4509.
      • The USA PATRIOT Act (Pub. L. 107-56, 2001) brought libraries within federal surveillance reach via Section 215 (FISA 'tangible things' business-records orders, 50 U.S.C. 1861) and Section 505 (National Security Letters, 18 U.S.C. 2709, with gag orders). The 2006 reauthorization and the 2015 USA FREEDOM Act added recipient challenge rights and curtailed bulk collection -- reforms that flowed directly from library-privacy litigation.
      • The Connecticut Four -- George Christian, Peter Chase, Barbara Bailey, and Jan Nocek of Library Connection -- received an NSL in 2005 and, with the ACLU, challenged it in Doe v. Gonzales. Judge Marrero struck down the NSL provisions; the gag was lifted in May 2006 and the FBI withdrew the NSL on June 26, 2006. They received the ALA's 2007 Paul Howard Award for Courage and the 2005 Robert B. Downs Intellectual Freedom Award.
      • Data retention is the decisive privacy variable: a library that does not retain what a patron read cannot disclose it under an NSL or Section 215 order. Automatic deletion of the patron-item link after return, session wiping on public computers, and short WiFi-log retention are therefore the strongest practical defenses against surveillance, beyond what statutes alone provide.
      • Third-party e-content vendors (OverDrive/Libby, hoopla, Kanopy, cloudLibrary) and cloud-hosted integrated library systems hold patron data outside the library's direct control and outside most state confidentiality statutes. ALA guidance urges libraries to negotiate privacy-protective contracts limiting collection, prohibiting secondary use, mandating deletion, and routing law-enforcement requests through the library.
      • Digital privacy tooling is now a library service: the Library Freedom Project (founded by Alison Macrina) trains librarians as Privacy Advocates and, with EFF and ACLU, has helped libraries deploy Tor relays -- most notably the Kilton Public Library (Lebanon, NH) exit-node controversy, where the board ultimately voted to keep the relay.

      Book Bans & Censorship in America

      The most comprehensive data available on book challenges, bans, and censorship legislation across the United States — from PEN America and ALA tracking data.

      Beginning in 2021, the United States experienced a dramatic and unprecedented escalation in book censorship. The American Library Association (ALA) and PEN America both documented record-breaking numbers of challenges and bans, with a 63% rise in reported cases between 2022 and 2023 alone. The wave intensified after 2021 and peaked in the 2023 reporting cycle, before moderating somewhat in 2024. About three-fourths of censored titles are books for children and young adults.

      Key Findings

      • The United States experienced an unprecedented surge in book censorship beginning in 2021: ALA documented 1,597 challenges in 2021, 2,571 in 2022, and a record 4,240 in 2023 (targeting 1,247 unique titles) -- a 63% rise from 2022 to 2023 alone, compared with approximately 275 in the pre-surge year 2020.
      • PEN America estimated over 10,000 book bans in U.S. public schools during the 30-month period from July 2021 through December 2023, across more than 180 school districts and 40 states, documented in its 'Banned in the USA' report series.
      • The censorship wave disproportionately targets marginalized voices: PEN America found that 36% of bans (2021-2023) targeted LGBTQ+ titles, 40% of banned books included characters of color, and 21% directly addressed race and racism -- meaning a majority of banned books center on the experiences of people of color or LGBTQ+ individuals.
      • School libraries account for 90.4% of documented challenges (18,904 of 20,890 in the Magnusson database), versus 7.3% in public libraries and 1.8% in college/university libraries, reflecting the greater vulnerability of school libraries to electoral pressure and state curriculum legislation.
      • Florida leads the nation with 5,798 documented challenges (1,139 books banned/removed), driven by the Parental Rights in Education Act (HB 1557, 2022), HB 1467 (2022), and the founding of Moms for Liberty in Florida in January 2021. Iowa has the highest ban rate (59.3%) and Tennessee the highest among high-volume states (74.3%).
      • Moms for Liberty, co-founded January 1, 2021 in Florida by Tina Descovich, Tiffany Justice, and Bridget Ziegler, grew to 285 chapters in 45 states by July 2023 and has been classified by the Southern Poverty Law Center as an 'anti-government extremist' group that 'advances an anti-student inclusion agenda.' Nearly 60% of its endorsed school board candidates lost their races by December 2023.
      • The Supreme Court's plurality opinion in Board of Education v. Pico (1982) -- that 'Local school boards may not remove books from school library shelves simply because they dislike the ideas contained in those books' -- remains the foundational precedent for book-ban litigation, and was cited in the 2023 federal lawsuit against Escambia County, Florida.
      • At least 15 states have enacted pro-censorship legislation (Florida, Texas, Missouri, Utah, Iowa, Tennessee, South Carolina, Oklahoma, Arkansas, Idaho, Virginia, Pennsylvania, Georgia, North Carolina, Kentucky), while at least 5 states (Illinois, Maryland, New Jersey, Vermont, Minnesota) have enacted anti-censorship laws protecting library collections from viewpoint-based removals.
      • The ALA's Office for Intellectual Freedom, founded after the 1939 Kern County ban of The Grapes of Wrath, is the primary national tracker of book challenges. ALA launched the 'Unite Against Book Bans' initiative in 2022 and organizes the annual Banned Books Week (founded 1982 by Judith Krug) to raise awareness of censorship.
      • State laws that criminalize or penalize librarians for providing books -- such as Missouri SB 775 (class A misdemeanor) and the vetoed Idaho HB 666 (would have created criminal liability) -- have a documented chilling effect: Missouri saw immediate, large-scale removals after SB 775's passage, and Idaho's governor vetoed HB 666 citing its 'chilling' effects.

      Library Impact & Outcomes

      What difference do libraries actually make? This section synthesizes research on the measurable economic, educational, and social impact of public libraries.

      Economic Impact

      Public libraries are one of the highest-return public investments in the United States. Multiple state and municipal return-on-investment (ROI) studies conducted between 2004 and 2018 consistently find that every $1 invested in public library services returns between $3.00 and $6.54 in direct and indirect economic value to the community. Libraries generate this return through direct employment, workforce development, small-business support, technology access that enables employment, educational services that raise human capital, and the consumer surplus of free borrowing. With total operating expenditures near $14.1 billion (FY2022) serving a service population above 334 million, the library system returns tens of billions of dollars in measurable community value annually.
      Overview

      Educational Impact

      • Overview: Public libraries are the most ubiquitous out-of-school educational institution in the United States, serving learners from infancy through adulthood. They are critical infrastructure for early literacy, summer learning, homework support, and adult basic education. Research on the 'summer slide' shows that students, especially those from low-income families, can lose two to three months of reading achievement over the summer break; library summer reading programs are a primary community response to this loss. With roughly 2.45 million children's programs and over 105 million total program attendees reported nationally (FY2022), libraries deliver educational programming at a scale rivaled only by the public school system itself.

      Key Studies

      • U.S. IMPACT Study (Opportunity for All) — 77 million Americans age 14+ (33%) used a library computer or Wi-Fi network in the prior year; education, employment, and health were leading uses.
      • ALA Public Library Funding & Technology Access Study (PLFTAS) — 92% of libraries provide job resources, 76% help with online job applications, 96% assist with e-government; 62% of unemployed Americans used librarie
      • Florida Statewide Economic Benefits Study — Florida public libraries returned $6.54 for every $1 invested, the highest documented library ROI.
      • Pennsylvania Public Library Economic Impact — $5.50 return per $1 invested.
      • South Carolina Public Library Economic Impact — $2.86 direct and $4.50 total economic impact per $1 invested.
      • Wisconsin Public Library Economic Contribution — $4.06 return per $1; quantified direct, indirect, and induced effects.
      • Cooper et al. Summer Learning Loss Meta-Analysis — Students lose about one month of overall learning and two to three months of reading achievement over summer; losses largest for low-income students.
      • Alexander, Entwisle, & Olson - Summer Learning and Achievement Gap — Two-thirds of the ninth-grade socioeconomic achievement gap is explained by unequal summer learning.
      • Pew Research Center - Library Attitudes Surveys — About 90% of Americans say public libraries are important to their communities; parents and lower-income Americans rely more heavily on libraries for
      • IMLS Public Libraries Survey (PLS) — National data on visits, circulation, programs, staffing, and funding; FY2024 recorded about 869 million visits, 1.7 billion circulation, and 5.09 mil

      Key Findings

      • Public libraries return between $3.00 and $6.54 for every $1 invested, making them among the highest-return public investments measured (state ROI studies 2004-2018).
      • 77 million Americans age 14+ used a library computer or Wi-Fi network in a single year; education (44%), employment (40%), and health (37%) were the leading uses (U.S. IMPACT Study 2010).
      • Libraries directly employ roughly 370,560 workers and operate on about $14.1 billion in annual expenditure while generating an estimated $40-45 billion in community value.
      • 92% of public libraries provide job-seeking resources and 76% help with online job applications, making libraries the most widespread free workforce infrastructure in the nation (PLFTAS).
      • Research shows low-income students lose two to three months of reading each summer; library summer reading programs are the most widely available community response, and two-thirds of the ninth-grade achievement gap traces to unequal summer learning.
      • Library use is remarkably equitable across racial groups (58-72% usage); Black and Hispanic Americans use library computers at higher rates than white Americans, underscoring libraries' role in bridging access gaps.
      • About 90% of public libraries offer free meeting space; roughly 2,000+ serve as passport acceptance facilities, making libraries core civic infrastructure.
      • San Francisco Public Library pioneered the social-worker-in-libraries model in 2009; dozens of cities now embed clinical social services for unhoused patrons.

      Libraries & Climate Change

      From hurricanes to wildfires, climate change poses growing threats to library buildings and collections. Libraries are also becoming community resilience hubs in the climate crisis.

      Libraries as Resilience Hubs

      • Definition: A resilience hub is a trusted, community-serving facility that supports residents everyday, during disruptions, and in recovery -- providing power, climate control, connectivity, water, communications, and program space. Libraries are natural resilience hubs: they are accessible, trusted, have HVAC and internet, long hours, professional staff, and no purchase-required barrier.
      • Framework Origins: The 'resilience hub' concept was advanced by the Urban Sustainability Directors Network (USDN) and adapted for libraries by the Urban Libraries Council (ULC) and ALA's Sustainability Round Table (SustainRT). ULC's resilience work provides member systems a framework and peer learning emphasizing equity-centered resilience and integration into city climate-adaptation planning.
      • Confidence: high

      Key Findings

      • Climate change poses direct, escalating threats to U.S. public libraries through five intersecting hazards -- flood, hurricane, wildfire smoke, extreme heat, and sea level rise -- with coastal and river-town central branches disproportionately exposed.
      • Libraries are repeat players in U.S. disaster response: they served as charging stations, internet lifelines, and FEMA-filing assistance points across Katrina (2005, $125B damage), Sandy (2012, ~$70B), Harvey (2017, $125B), the 2018 Camp Fire, and the 2021 Kentucky tornadoes.
      • Hurricane Helene (2024) underscored libraries' role as connectivity infrastructure: with telecom down for weeks in western North Carolina, library Wi-Fi became essential for FEMA registration and family contact.
      • Tornadoes have outright destroyed library buildings (Joplin 2011 EF5, Moore 2013 EF5, Mayfield 2021 EF4), but in each case the rebuilt library anchored community recovery -- e.g., Joplin's new ~$8.5M building opened in 2017.
      • The 'resilience hub' model, advanced by the Urban Sustainability Directors Network and adapted for libraries by ULC and ALA's SustainRT, formalizes what libraries already do; cooling-center and warming-center designation is its most common institutionalization.
      • ALA institutionalized sustainability through SustainRT (first meeting July 1, 2013; officers elected March 2014) and the 2015 Council Resolution on the Importance of Sustainable Libraries, establishing sustainability as a core professional value.
      • LEED certification of public libraries took off after Fayetteville's Blair Library (LEED Silver, 2004, named Library Journal's Library of the Year 2005); high-profile examples include San Mateo (Gold, 2006), Seattle Central (Silver, 2004), and Austin Central (Gold, 2017, with a 373 kW solar array and one of the largest living roofs on a U.S. library).
      • The seed-library movement, sparked by Richmond Grows (Richmond, CA, 2010), has grown to an estimated 500+ U.S. seed libraries, often supported by Master Gardener/Extension partners -- making libraries direct contributors to local food security and biodiversity.

      American Library History Timeline

      From the colonial subscription libraries of the 1700s to today's digital hubs — the complete chronological story of American libraries.

      Key Figures in Library History

      NameEraContribution
      Thomas Bray
      Benjamin Franklin
      Justin Winsor
      Melvil Dewey
      Charles Ammi Cutter
      Samuel Swett Green
      Andrew Carnegie
      John Cotton Dana
      Herbert Putnam
      Archibald MacLeish
      Luther H. Evans
      Belle da Costa Greene
      Mary Lemist Titcomb
      Enoch Pratt
      Ernestine Rose
      Arturo (Arthur) Schomburg
      Thomas Fountain Blue
      Dorothy Porter Wesley
      Augusta Braxton Baker
      Regina Anderson

      Key Findings

      • U.S. library history begins with private, church, and bequest collections; Captain Robert Keayne's 1656 Boston book bequest is the earliest colonial library precursor, and Thomas Bray's ~70 parish/provincial libraries (1695-1704) formed the first coordinated colonial library network.
      • Benjamin Franklin's Library Company of Philadelphia (1731) was the first successful subscription library in the colonies and the model for the 'library company' social library form that spread nationwide; it remains in operation.
      • The transition to tax-supported public libraries culminated in the Peterborough, New Hampshire town library (1833, first tax-supported public library in the world) and the Boston Public Library (founded 1848, opened 1854, first large municipally supported free public library in the U.S.).
      • Boston Public Library achieved multiple U.S. 'firsts': first to open free to the public and allow borrowing (1854), first branch library (East Boston, 1870), and first dedicated children's room (1895).
      • The 19th century diversified library types: New York authorized school district libraries (1835), mercantile libraries served urban clerks and workers, and YMCA libraries reached immigrants and travelers, while the ALA (1876) and Dewey Decimal Classification (1876) professionalized the field.
      • Andrew Carnegie's library program funded 1,689 public library buildings in the United States (2,509 worldwide; 1883-1929), totaling about $45.86 million across 1,419 public grants plus 109 academic grants. Carnegie required municipalities to provide land and commit annual maintenance at ~10% of the grant, embedding tax support in local budgets.
      • The Carnegie program standardized small-town library architecture through efficient, open-stack plans with Classical Revival styling; many Carnegie buildings still operate as libraries and several are on the National Register of Historic Places.
      • Mary Lemist Titcomb launched the first U.S. bookwagon at Washington County Free Library, Hagerstown, Maryland, in 1905, pioneering county-wide rural service and the bookmobile model.

      Libraries & Public Health

      Libraries are increasingly on the front lines of public health — from social workers embedded in branches to naloxone distribution to telehealth access points.

      Mental Health Services

      • Overview: Public libraries are frequent first points of contact for people experiencing mental illness, substance use, trauma, and behavioral health crises -- in part because they are among the few free, drop-i

      Libraries & the Opioid Crisis

      • Overview: The United States is in an ongoing opioid epidemic described by doctors and researchers as 'one of the most devastating public health catastrophes of our time.' It unfolded in three waves: t
      • Research Note: A decision-analytic model cited in the opioid-epidemic literature estimated that under the status quo about 484,429 individuals would die of fatal opioid overdose between 2020 and 2029, but that combi

      • Overview: Embedding social workers in public libraries is a response to the rising complexity of patron needs -- especially unhoused patrons, people with mental illness and substance-use disorders, and families

      Key Findings

      • Libraries function as social determinants of health. SDOH research finds more than half of health is determined by social (non-clinical) factors; libraries act on multiple SDOH domains at once -- education, employment, healthcare access, built environment, and food security -- through a single trusted, free institution.
      • San Francisco Public Library pioneered library-based social work in 2009 (Leah Esguerra), launching a movement that has spread to dozens of U.S. systems and made social workers a recognized library service model.
      • Mental Health First Aid, developed in Australia in 2001 and now in 25+ countries with 6M+ trained (as of 2024), is the leading structured training library systems use to prepare staff for patron mental-health and substance-use crises.
      • Naloxone in libraries is a worker-safety and public-health intervention: naloxone (Narcan), approved for U.S. opioid-overdose use in 1971 and now OTC, reverses overdose within minutes, and library staff are frequently overdose bystanders.
      • Libraries were essential COVID-19 public-health infrastructure -- hosting testing and vaccination, distributing PPE, sustaining remote learning, and keeping Wi-Fi on (98% of public libraries offered free Wi-Fi per ALA, 2015) even while buildings were closed.
      • MedlinePlus and the NNLM, both run by the National Library of Medicine, are the backbone of library consumer-health service: MedlinePlus launched in 1998 and reached about 400 million users worldwide in 2015.
      • Broadband is the precondition for library health work. Telehealth requires strong broadband; libraries supply it near-universally and are therefore natural telehealth access points, especially for rural, older, and low-income patrons with the worst home connectivity.
      • Partnerships, not libraries alone, deliver most library health programs. Libraries provide venue, trust, broadband, and outreach; public health departments, hospitals, FQHCs, NNLM, and MSW programs provide clinical expertise, funding, and liability.

As of January 2025 the United States had 53.3 million foreign-born residents (15.8% of the population), both record highs; in 2017 about 45% of the foreign-born were naturalized citizens and 27% were lawful permanent residents eligible to naturalize - the exact population library citizenship programs target.'}
      • {'finding': "REFORMA has anchored Latino and Spanish-language library service for over 50 years.       • {'finding': "Queens Public Library is a national model for immigrant service. Its 62 branches, 7 Adult Learning Centers, 2 Family Literacy Centers, the Flushing International Resource Center (collections in 12+ languages), and the tagline 'We speak your language' demonstrate how a single system can integrate ESL, citizenship, multilingual collections, foreign-credential assistance, and digital literacy."}
      • {'finding': 'Library legal services depend on a strict referral boundary. Libraries do not give legal advice; they host clinics and refer patrons to attorneys and to DOJ EOIR Accredited Representatives working through Recognized Organizations (8 C.F.R. Part 1292). This referral architecture is essential to fighting notario fraud, a chronic problem for immigrant patrons.'}
      • {'finding': "The Library Bill of Rights is the textual foundation for sanctuary and welcoming library policies. Article V - that a person's right to use a library should not be denied or abridged because of origin, age, background, or views - directly undergirds service to immigrants regardless of status, and Article VII protects the privacy of immigrant patrons' library use."}
      • {'finding': 'Naturalization ceremonies at libraries make the institution part of the citizenship path. Hosting federal judges who administer the Oath of Allegiance - especially around Constitution Day and Citizenship Day (September 17) - signals that the library where an immigrant studied for the civics test is also where they become a citizen.'}
      • {'finding': 'Multilingual collections are concentrated in a few large systems. While Spanish is collected widely, deep multilingual holdings (dozens of languages) are found mainly at NYPL, QPL, LAPL, and similar urban systems; smaller and rural systems often lack the multilingual staffing and vendor access to maintain non-English collections, leaving many immigrant communities underserved.'}

      • {'finding': 'Libraries are legally designated voter-registration sites.       • {'finding': 'Libraries are everyday polling infrastructure. Although no single national count of library polling places is published, libraries are among the most common public buildings used as polling sites because they are ADA-accessible, centrally located, publicly owned, and trusted -- qualities election boards prioritize.'}
      • {'finding': 'The 2020 census made libraries indispensable to the decennial count.       • {'finding': "E-Rate is the connectivity backbone for library civic roles.       • {'finding': "Libraries are the on-ramp to digital democracy. As government services and civic participation move online, the digital divide becomes a civic-engagement divide; libraries' public computers, WiFi, hotspot lending, and digital navigators are what make e-government and online civic participation possible for unconnected residents."}

      Libraries & Homelessness

      Public libraries are among the few public indoor spaces open to all without expectation of purchase, making them de facto daytime shelters for people experiencing homelessness. A growing number of systems now embed professional social workers and peer support specialists to connect vulnerable patrons with housing, health, and benefits services.

      Public libraries are among the most accessible public institutions in the United States: they are free, open without appointment, do not require proof of address or income for basic use, and are located in nearly every community. For people experiencing homelessness, the library is frequently one of the few indoor public spaces available during daytime hours for rest, warmth or cooling, restroom access, internet and charging, and connection to services. There is no single national registry of homeless library patrons, so precise counts do not exist; the figures below combine HUD national point-in-time counts of homelessness with library-system surveys and patron-encounter counts reported by individual systems.

      National Context

      {'source': 'U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD), Annual Homeless Assessment Report (AHAR)', 'pit_count_january_2024': "More than 770,000 people were identified as experiencing homelessness on a single night in January 2024, according to HUD's annual point-in-time count - a record high and an increase of more than 18 percent over the prior year.", 'pit_count_2023': 'In 2023, the federal point-in-time survey identified a record 653,104 people experiencing homelessness, a 12 percent jump over the previous year - described as roughly quadruple any other single-year increase on record, with increases in every category and demographic of homelessness.', 'unsheltered_share_note': 'A substantial and growing share of the homeless population is unsheltered (living outdoors, in vehicles, or in places not meant for human habitation). Unsheltered homelessness has risen steadily since 2016, particularly on the West Coast, which corresponds to the regions where library systems most frequently report large daytime populations of unhoused patrons.', 'family_and_youth_2024': 'In 2024, children under 18 experienced the largest single-year increase in homelessness of any group, with nearly 150,000 children identified as homeless on survey night. Overall family homelessness rose approximately 39 percent year-over-year.', 'supreme_court_context': 'In June 2024, the U.S. Supreme Court ruled in City of Grants Pass v. Johnson that anti-camping ordinances do not constitute cruel and unusual punishment under the Eighth Amendment even when no shelter bed is available, allowing cities to enforce laws against sleeping and camping in public spaces. This ruling has implications for libraries, which may see displaced encampment populations seek daytime refuge indoors.'}

      Social Workers in Libraries

      Beginning with the San Francisco Public Library in 2009, a growing number of U.S. public library systems have embedded professional social workers and peer support specialists on library staff to connect patrons experiencing homelessness, mental illness, addiction, and poverty to services. The embedded-social-worker model reframes the library's response to complex patron needs from a policing/security posture to a public-health and social-services posture. Library social workers typically conduct outreach on the floor, build relationships with regular patrons, make warm referrals to housing, benefits, mental-health, and substance-use services, and train library staff in de-escalation and trauma-informed service.

      {'library': 'San Francisco Public Library (SFPL)', 'year_started': 2009, 'pioneer': 'Leah Esguerra', 'note': "In 2009, at the San Francisco Public Library's Main Library, Leah Esguerra became the first library-based social worker in the United States. SFPL operates a 'homeless and poverty outreach library team' in partnership with the San Francisco Department of Public Health, helping find housing and other services for homeless patrons. The SFPL model is widely credited with originating the embedded-social-worker movement in libraries and has been profiled by the SF Chronicle, NPR, and the library trade press.", 'source': "Wikipedia, 'San Francisco Public Library' (citing SF Chronicle reporting); SFPL website"}

      Services Offered

      Beyond connecting patrons to external services, many libraries have begun offering or hosting direct services that address the basic daily needs of people experiencing homelessness - needs that, if unmet, make library use and broader stability difficult. These services typically originate in recognition that patrons cannot job-search, read, or access services when they lack access to showers, clean clothes, mail delivery, a mailing address, or safe storage for belongings.

      Key Findings

      • Libraries function as de facto daytime shelters for people experiencing homelessness in nearly every U.S. community, especially in urban central libraries where 10-30 percent of daytime visitors may be housing-unstable. This role is largely unfunded and unstaffed as a shelter function, creating persistent operational tension.
      • The San Francisco Public Library originated the library-based social worker model in 2009 with Leah Esguerra, and this model has since spread to dozens of U.S. public library systems, shifting the library's response to homelessness from a policing posture to a public-health and social-services posture.
      • Peer navigator programs - staff with lived experience of homelessness or behavioral-health challenges - are an increasingly common and effective library model. The DC Public Library's Peer Support Specialists, certified through the DC Department of Behavioral Health, hold regular hours at multiple branches and help patrons find shelter, housing, treatment, ID, and reentry support.
      • National homelessness reached record levels in 2023 (653,104 people on the January 2023 point-in-time count, a 12 percent jump) and again in 2024 (more than 770,000, an 18 percent single-year increase), with the largest increase among children under 18 (nearly 150,000) and a 39 percent rise in family homelessness. Rising homelessness directly increases demand on libraries' de facto shelter and social-service functions.
      • The ALA has explicit, Council-adopted policy guidance on serving people experiencing poverty and homelessness: Policy Manual section B.8.10 ('Addressing Poverty, Economic Inequality, and the Responsibilities of Libraries,' formerly 'Library Services to the Poor,' amended January 27, 2019) sets out 20 policy objectives including removal of fees, review of conduct policies for unintentional barriers, and use of person-first language.
      • The 'Economic Barriers to Library Access' interpretation of the Library Bill of Rights (amended 2025) establishes that library resources should be readily and equitably accessible to all users and that libraries should eliminate financial barriers - a foundational equity principle that undergirds free library service to homeless patrons.
      • Restroom access is the most basic and most contested library service for homeless patrons and a flashpoint for equity. Policies that lock restrooms, require codes, or limit restroom time impose burdens that fall disproportionately on unhoused patrons, while real challenges (overdose, vandalism, maintenance) drive restrictions. Many systems now pair restroom access with naloxone availability and staff overdose-response training.
      • The 1992 federal case Kreimer v. Bureau of Police of the Town of Morristown established that public libraries are limited public fora and that patrons have a First Amendment right of access, while libraries may enforce content-neutral conduct rules. This precedent continues to shape library behavior policies and limits the ability to exclude patrons based on homeless status.
      • Libraries are critical warming and cooling centers during extreme weather, and this role is growing with climate change. Many municipal emergency plans explicitly list libraries as designated warming/cooling sites, and ALA Policy Manual B.8.11 urges governments to support libraries' role in emergency response.
      • The 2024 Supreme Court decision in City of Grants Pass v. Johnson, allowing cities to enforce anti-camping laws even when no shelter is available, may increase daytime displacement of encampment populations into libraries, expanding libraries' de facto shelter burden precisely as homelessness hits record highs.

      Libraries & Literacy

      From early literacy storytimes to adult reading instruction, digital skills classes, and summer reading programs that combat learning loss, libraries are the nation's most accessible literacy infrastructure — open to all ages, free of charge, in nearly every community.

      National Literacy Statistics

      Adult Literacy

      Adult literacy refers to the reading, writing, numeracy, and problem-solving skills adults use in daily life, work, and civic participation. Public libraries are a primary, no-barrier entry point for adults seeking to improve these skills, offering one-on-one tutoring, small-group classes, high-interest low-reading-level (hi-lo) collections, GED preparation support, and referrals to partner adult education providers.

      Early Literacy

      Early literacy describes what children know about reading and writing before they can actually read and write. It is built through language exposure, book handling, letter knowledge, and the five ECRR practices. Libraries are the primary free provider of early-literacy programming (storytimes, baby lapsits, toddler read-alouds) for families of all income levels.

      {'name': 'Every Child Ready to Read (ECRR)', 'partners': 'Public Library Association (PLA) and Association for Library Service to Children (ALSC), divisions of ALA', 'first_edition': '2004', 'second_edition': '2011', 'research_base': "Grounded in the National Research Council report 'Preventing Reading Difficulties in Young Children' and in early brain-development research showing that language exposure in the first years of life shapes later reading success.", 'five_practices': ['Talking - conversation builds vocabulary and background knowledge.', 'Singing - develops phonological awareness by slowing language into syllables and rhyme.', 'Reading - shared reading is the single most important way to help children get ready to read.', 'Writing - fine-motor and letter-recognition activities support print awareness.', 'Playing - dramatic and hands-on play reinforce narrative skills.'], 'early_literacy_components': ['phonological awareness', 'vocabulary', 'print awareness', 'print motivation', 'letter knowledge', 'narrative skills']}

      Digital Literacy

      Digital literacy is the ability to use computers, the internet, and software to find, evaluate, create, and communicate information. Public libraries are the most widespread free provider of public computing and internet access in the United States, making them central to closing the digital divide and to digital-skills education.

      {'name': 'Northstar Digital Literacy', 'origin': 'Literacy Minnesota (formerly Minnesota Literacy Council)', 'description': 'Northstar defines the basic skills needed to perform tasks on computers and online. It provides standards, online assessments, and a curriculum used by adult education programs, libraries, workforce centers, and nonprofits.', 'coverage': 'Northstar is used in all 50 U.S. states and in other countries; learners can earn certificates by passing proctored assessments at approved test sites.', 'modules': ['Basic Computer Use', 'Internet Basics', 'Using Email', 'Windows', 'Mac OS', 'Word Processing (Word)', 'Spreadsheets (Excel)', 'Presentations (PowerPoint)', 'Information Literacy', 'Social Media', 'Your Digital Footprint', 'Managing Your Digital Footprint', 'Technology Basics (using mobile devices)']}

      Summer Reading

      Summer reading programs combat the 'summer slide' - the well-documented loss of reading skills during school breaks, which disproportionately affects low-income children who lack access to books and enrichment. Public libraries run the largest free summer reading programming network in the United States.

      Studies of summer learning loss show that children from lower-income households can lose two to three months of reading achievement over the summer while higher-income peers often gain; cumulative losses contribute to the achievement gap.

      Citizenship / naturalization corner programsPublic libraries (often with USCIS partnerships)ESL / civic literacyCivics and English preparation for the U.S. naturalization test, reinforcing reading and language skills for immigrants.

      Key Findings

      • Roughly 1 in 5 U.S. adults read at the lowest literacy level.
      • Libraries are the most accessible free literacy infrastructure in the country.
      • Early literacy is built at home before school, and libraries equip caregivers to do it.
      • Family literacy breaks intergenerational cycles.
      • Summer reading programs meaningfully combat the summer slide.
      • Digital literacy is now a core literacy, and libraries lead its delivery.
      • Information and media literacy are essential civic skills in an era of misinformation.
      • ESL learners are a large and overlapping segment of adult literacy learners.
      • Partnerships multiply the impact of library literacy work.

      Libraries Serving Veterans

      Public libraries serve millions of U.S. veterans and military families — offering VA claims assistance, GI Bill information, veteran book clubs, oral history projects in partnership with the Library of Congress Veterans History Project, and connections to benefits and crisis resources.

      Understanding the size and distribution of the veteran population is essential to planning library veteran services. The U.S. veteran population is large, aging, and geographically concentrated, with significant implications for which library systems serve the most veteran patrons.

      National Veteran Population

      {'approximate_total': '~21 million American veterans (as of 2017, per the Wikipedia veteran article citing VA data)', 'veteran_parent_decline': 'In 1990, 40% of young Americans had a veteran for a parent; this decreased to 16% in 2014, reflecting the post-draft all-volunteer force and the aging of the Vietnam-era veteran cohort.', 'millennial_veteran_share': 'According to the Pew Research Center, only 4% of male millennials (born 1981-96) are veterans, compared with 47% of men in their 70s and 80s - many of whom came of age during the Korean War and its aftermath.', 'foreign_born': 'In 2016, of the veterans born outside the United States, Mexican and Filipino Americans made up the two largest populations, with 3% of all veterans having been born outside the United States.'}

      VA Partnerships

      The U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) is a Cabinet-level executive department charged with providing lifelong healthcare and benefits to eligible military veterans. It operates 170 VA medical centers and outpatient clinics and, as of June 2020, employed roughly 412,892 people. Public libraries partner with multiple VA components - chiefly the Veterans Benefits Administration (VBA), the Veterans Health Administration (VHA), and the consumer-facing VA.gov (formerly Vets.gov) portal - to connect veterans with benefits, healthcare, and crisis support. These partnerships let the library serve as a community access point for a federal agency whose own facilities, while national in scale, are not present in every town.

      • VBA: {'name': 'Veterans Benefits Administration (VBA)', 'role': "VBA administers the VA's non-medical benefit programs: disability compensation, pension, survivors&#
      • VHA: {'name': 'VA Medical Centers (VHA)', 'role': 'The Veterans Health Administration is the largest division of the VA and the second-largest in the entire federal gover
      • Crisis Line: {'name': 'Veterans Crisis Line', 'contact': 'Dial 988 then Press 1; text 838255; chat online', 'description': 'The Veterans Crisis Line connects

      Veterans History Project

      Veterans history projects at libraries preserve the firsthand recollections of U.S. military veterans so that future generations may hear directly from those who served. The cornerstone national effort is the Library of Congress Veterans History Project (VHP), and public libraries serve as both VHP partners and independent oral-history collectors. Through interviews, memoirs, letters, photographs, and diaries, libraries help build the historic record of American wartime service.

      {'name': 'Library of Congress Veterans History Project (VHP)', 'parent': 'American Folklife Center, Library of Congress', 'authorizing_legislation': "Public Law 106-380, the Veterans' Oral History Project Act, enacted October 27, 2000", 'sponsors': 'Representatives Ron Kind, Amo Houghton, and Steny Hoyer in the U.S. House; Senators Max Cleland and Chuck Hagel in the U.S. Senate; signed into law by President Bill Clinton', 'mission': 'To collect, preserve, and make accessible the personal narratives of U.S. military veterans who served from World War I through more recent conflicts and peacekeeping missions, so that future generations may hear directly from veterans and better understand what they saw, did, and felt during their service.', 'scale': "Through 2010 the VHP held more than 65,000 collections and was considered the largest oral-history program of its kind in the nation. Collections include video and audio interviews, original letters, diaries, photographs, memoirs, and historic documents related to a veteran's wartime service.", 'directors': ['2000-2006: Ellen McCulloch-Lovell', '2006-2016: Robert Patrick', '2016-present: Karen Lloyd'], 'anniversaries': ["10th anniversary (2010): commemorative event with Librarian of Congress James H. Billington calling on Americans to collect veterans' stories.", "15th anniversary (2015): 'VHP at 15: Collections Over the Years' web presentation in the 'Experiencing War' series.", '20th anniversary (2020): online concerts and panels, November 6-14, 2020, featuring Operation Song songwriters and veteran-participant discussions.']}

      Veteran Services at Libraries

      Beyond programs and collections, many libraries offer specific, transactional veteran services that connect patrons directly to benefits and assistance. These services address the practical barriers veterans face in accessing earned benefits: complex federal forms, online-only portals, long travel distances to VA regional offices, and the need for accredited help with claims.

      ALA Veterans Initiatives

      The American Library Association (ALA) is the primary national professional body guiding library services to veterans and military families. ALA's engagement with military service dates to World War I, when the ALA Executive Board, initiated by Walter Lewis Brown, established the Library War Service Committee to supply books and periodicals to military personnel at home and overseas - an effort that also led to the founding of the American Library in Paris. Today, ALA's veteran-serving work is distributed across its Office for Diversity, Literacy and Outreach Services (ODLOS), the Association for Library Service to Children (ALSC), the Public Library Association (PLA), and the Public Programs Office.

      {'name': 'ALA Libraries Serve Veterans', 'description': "ALA promotes library service to veterans through resource guides, programming toolkits, and advocacy that frame veterans as a priority service population. ALA encourages libraries to commemorate Veterans Day (November 11) and Memorial Day with programs, exhibits, and book displays, and to build sustained year-round veteran services rather than one-off holiday events. The initiative draws on the Library Bill of Rights, whose Article V holds that 'A person's right to use a library should not be denied or abridged because of origin, age, background, or views' - a principle that undergirds service to veterans regardless of discharge status or era of service.", 'key_components': ['Veterans and military-families resource pages and subject guides on the ALA website.', 'Programming models for veteran book clubs, writing workshops, and oral-history projects tied to the Library of Congress VHP.', 'Advocacy for IMLS funding that supports veteran-serving library programs, including Grants to States.', 'Continuing education at ALA Annual and Midwinter conferences on serving veterans and military families.', 'Coordination with the Public Programs Office on veteran-themed exhibits and discussion programs.']}

      • {'finding': "Libraries are frontline veteran-service institutions. Combining benefits navigation, PTS/TBI consumer-health resources, veteran book clubs, writing workshops, VSO office hours, and oral-history recording, libraries serve as a single trusted community entry point for veterans navigating post-service life - a role reinforced by IMLS identifying 'Championing Veterans' as a distinct issue area."}
      • {'finding': 'The Library of Congress Veterans History Project anchors library veteran-history work. Created by Public Law 106-380 (signed October 27, 2000) and holding more than 65,000 collections by 2010, the VHP is the largest oral-history program of its kind in the nation and relies on a national network of libraries, schools, and VSOs to record interviews using the VHP Field Kit.'}
      • {'finding': "The VA's 56 regional benefits offices cannot reach every veteran alone. With only 56 VBA regional offices and 170 VA medical centers, the VA depends on community partners - including libraries - to host claims clinics, VSO hours, and telehealth access points that bridge geographic gaps, especially for rural veterans."}
      • {'finding': "Military OneSource officially links libraries to DoD family-readiness infrastructure.
